Tea a la Sir Andrew Clarke.

- - For years, whenever my Customers have asked me for a Mild Tea, I have advised the Pure. old Fashioned China which / keep, and the advice has been appreciated It is very satisfactory to them that such an eminent authority as Sir Andrew Clarke, the London Physician, corroborates opinion. Here is an extract from an interesting lecture, delivered by Sir Andrew the other day, to the Students of the London Hospital : Tea is a blessed beverage. I donotknovr what I should do without it. But there is tea and tee, ansl one of the teas I " have in my mind is the representation of all that is physiologically wicked. Igo about town a good deal holding consulta- tions here and there, and about five o'clock, when I get into a place, the lady of the house will say to me • Sir And has ur, and she you look tired, do let me give you a cup of tea.' I say' thank you very much. But the tea stood for half-an-ho' em rks, I know you do you not like It strong, Sir Andrew,' and she then pours about a tablespoonful of the tea into a cup arndafills thecup up with water. Now I call it positivecruelty to give tea like that to anybody and I hope you, gentlemen, ace against it. Tea, to be useful, should be first of au Blank China Tea ; the Indian Tee which is will always set your f be cultivated has become so powertut in its effects upon the nery me system that a cup of it taken early in the morning, as many do, so disorders the nervous system that those who take It actually get into a state of tea intoxication, and It produces a form of nervous disturbance, which is most painful to witness. It you want to have, either for yourselves or for your patients, tea which will not injure and which will refresh, get Slack China Tea, putting in the right measure—the old fashioned teaspoonful for each person and one for the blessed pot. Then pour en briskly boiling water, and within five minutes you must pour It off again, or it will become wicked instead of good?' Such is Sir Andrew Clarke's opinion and advise. A few words Of warning, however, are necessary to those who have for- gotten what this old fashioned tea is like. The colour of the tea after it is brewed will he found tobe much paler than that now in use, and the taste may not be acceptable at first owing to its apparent insipidness ; but if its use is persisted in, it will very soon be found to be much more suitable for the er•fem. an I if, in a week to two after its use, a trial is again made of the kind now in ordinary use, you will wonder however you could have been using it,and you will endorse Sir Andrew's statement. TIT-BITS.

TUE PROFESSOR STUMPED.

It was a Professor of Legerdemain of whom / speak. He was exhibiting at Conway, and he had a goodly audience. Those far country villages are the places for real enjoyment of such things. The honest, plodding people are not satiated with amuse- ment, and when, semi-occasionally, a good show comes along they turn out en mane, taking all hands with them. On the present occasion the professor was going to perform the wonderful trick of causing a piece of money to pass, by the simple effort of his will from a securely-locked box upon the table, or from a gentleman's hand, into the pocket of one of the boys in the audience. Of course he must call up a boy to help him; and he chanced to fix his eye upon a tow-headed urchin near the front, who promised, in appearance, to answer his purpose, He called, and the boy came up. "Now, my man," said the professor, in his grandiose way, at the same time laying his hand upon the boy's head, "I am going to cause that piece of money—you see it ?—it is a solid piece of metal,—to pass from that box, in which you shall see me put it, into yom pocket. You don't think I can do it, do you ?" "No, sir,—l don't !," answered the lad, with de- cided emphasis. "Well,—do you -stand up here, and we shall see." "But, sir," persisted the boy, " ther' stint no use 'r yer tryin', 'cause I know you can't do it. "You know I can't? Don't be too sure. Wait and see. Just you stand right here—there! Now hold up your head and look steadily at use, to see that I do not cheat you." "0, well !" muttered the persistent urchin, with a comical twist of the freckled face, "I'll stand anywhere you want, only, 'f 'u git any money inter my pocket, I reckon you'll hey ter find the pocket, for I aiut had sich a thing this two months. I tored 'em out a hookin' apples, and haint had none put in sence. I haint dared t' ask 'em ter do it, d' you see ?" The pocketless hero was applauded vociferously. It was rich and rare, and helped to make up for much that would have been lacking, of fun and interest, without it.

AN HONEST COWARD.

On the Peninsula, during the "unpleasantness," while McClellan was with drawing from his ad- vanced post towards Richmond, on the Chicka- hominy, there was considerable heavy fighting and skirmishing going on continuously. At Savage's Station, on the morning of the 29th of June, an attack was made upon the extreme front—Gen. Sumner's corps. Very likely the man of whom we are to tell had just eaten a good hearty breakfast, and did not care to lose the benefit of it. At all events, while that morning a attack was in progress, and the brigadiers were hurrying up their regi- ments, the colonel of one of the regiments engaged found one of his men skulking under a safe cover, entirely out of the way of the enemy's missiles. The colonel ordered the an to "come out of that!" and in language which we need not tran- scribe; but the man refused flatly. "What ! " yelled the colonel, in sheer amaze- ment at the fellow's audacity.—His cowardice was bad enough, but this direct and emphatic refusal to obey was unbearable.—" Come out of that instantly, or I'll shoot you !" "Shoot away, Colonel. I sha'n't come!' "You skulking rascal ! what do you mean? Would you rather be shot skulking there like a coward than come out and fight like a man? By the life of me I'll fire, if you don't come !" "Fire away as quick as you please," returned the incorrigible. "I'd rather run the risk of the bullets in that old pea-shooter of yourn than stand right in the way of a thousand, or more, toinie balls! D'you see—l take the chances. Shoot away 1" In disgust the colonel turned away, not caring to have the poor fellow's blood on his hands, and, moreover, being needed at his post. He had taken but a few steps, however, when a heavy thud —a yell of fear, and then a crash and a roar—burst upon his ears, and On looking hack, he saw that a shell had fallen close by the skulking man's side, bursting as it struck the earth, and literally rend- ing the coward piecemeal ! A more terrible thing, even, than a minie ball had found him in his place of hiding.

NERVE AND RUMOUR IN BATTLE,

A brave English sailor, at the battle of Tras ralgar, while serving at his gun on the main-deck, was struck by a shot, coming in at the port, which took off his leg below the knee. As he sank down upon a shot-box, and saw the section of limb clean gone, he muttered : "Ah ! it's only a shilling affair, that I—had it gone a few inches higher I'd a got my eighteen penoe for it" He alluded to the scale of pensions, as graded by the severity of the wound. Afterwards, while one of his mates was carry- ing him below, to the cockpit, he suddenly cried out: "Ho ! Jack! when you go bach, be sure and take a look at my leg, and save me the silver buckle on the shoe. I'll do as much for you, if the Lord spares me !" Here is a good reply from another brave Britian —This time a Captain. It was when Admiral Lord St. Vincent's fleet was bearing down upon the fleet of the enemy—the Spaniards—that the chaplain of a frigate ask the commander, who was all alive with excitement, and eager in the work: "Captain, have you reckoned the number of the enemy?' "so, no,—not yet," replied the brave chief. 4' We can do that more readily after they are ours I" Here is another of a different cast, but its humour is not to be denied: The French, at Wagram, were making the first onset of battle, when a sergeant of infantry, who was holding his company in line, upon the extreme right of his regiment, was set upon by a yelping, snarling cur, belonging to the colonel. The old soldier could not tamely bear this, and, with a movement like lightning, he charged bayonet upon the dog, and ran him through, probably to the heart. The colonel saw, and being near to the spot, be rode up. Look you, my man, why could you not have just as well made at my dog with the butt of your musket? " Parbien I I'd have done it, Colonel, if the brute had only made at me in the same way l"

ANECDOTE OF JOHN WILKES

It will be understood that John Wilkes, just pre. 'ions to the breaking out of the American Revolu- tion, had, by his terrific onslaughts upon the king, and the kingly power, made himself an idol of the London masses, Mad he been left alone to pursue his rabid political course as he pleased, the people might not have taken him to their bosoms; but when the lords and nobles, and all the puffy nabobs came to persecute the man, then the masses loved him. In his paper, called "The North Briton," he attacked the king with such virulence,—accusing the monach of falsehood,— that he was apprehended, under an order from the Court of King's Bench, and imprisoned in the Tower. While there, it was told through the city that he would come out and accept a high and re- sponsible office at the hands of the citizens. Hear- ing this a number of the city officials, having the good of the community at heart, waited upon Wilkes in his place of duress, and zxpostulated pith him upon the subject. "Really,' ' said the chief spokesman of the depu- tation, " think if we should take the sense of the eity, throughout, we could convince you A your mirror."

"Pshaw I" cried Wilkes, "Di take the nonsenee id the city, and beat you, two to one !" And I think they believed him.

CAPITAL" CHAT.

Mr. George is busily engaged on his treatise on political economy, and his son Henry George, jun., nas taken up his work in journalism. It was ru- moured that Mr. George, sen., was to be the Ameri- can Consul at Manchester, but he wrote to the papers saying that he would accept no office from the Government. "I want," he said; "nothing from the Cleveland Administration save the abolition of protection and its incidents, and I am good enough Democrat to want that badly."

Mr. S. M. Burroughs, an enthusiastic follower of Mr. Henry George, has arranged to publish every week a journal called the " Single Tax," printed in America, and intended to convey to English land reformers a record of the movement in America. The paper contains a great deal of information con- cerning the single tax campaign in the States, and shows that the movement is still making progress. A Single Tax International Congress will be held at the Chicago Exhibition in August next. 1 The death plainly announced in Tuesday's obitu- ary as "Charlotte, widow of John Collingwood Bruce," will bring to the recollection of the thou- sands of boys who have been educated at the once famons Percy-st., Academy, in Newcastle-on-Tyne, the cheery face of the ever-popular wife of the dis- tinguished Northumbrian schoolmaster, historian (Roman Wail, &c.), and antiquarian, who himself passed away only a few months ago, The old lady lived long enough to see her son, Sir Gainsford, a judge on the High Court Bench. It must have afforded her singular gratification to welcome him to his native city the other day, when he opeaed ' the assizes in the same court where he first won his laurels at the Bar over thirty years ago.

Germany has just lost a man of considerable note in relation to art in the death of Professor Lubke; whose works on the history of art have for many years been of world-wide repute, especially his " History of Architecture," his " History of the Plastic Arts„' and his two works on the Renais- sance of Art in Germany and in France. Lubke was born at Dortmund in 1826, and was consequently sixty-seven years of age. He was successively Pro- fessor of History in Relation to the Arts at Zurich, Stuttgart, and Carlsruhe, where he died. The last appointment dated from 1885. Professor Lubke was not only a man of great erudition but of singu- lar elevation and beauty of charactor.

Nikita has been allowing the impertinent inter- viewer to pry a little into her love affairs. She was caught at her hotel in Paris, superintending the packing of trunk upon trunk of new costumes for her Chicago engagement of a hundred nights. " Are they not beautiful 7" she said joyously, as she shook out one or two for inspection. "You see I want them to be very beautiful, for it is eight years since I left America, with long hair and short clothes. It will be such fun to go back grown up in French gowns. Oh, yes," she added," It's quite true that I'm engaged to a Persian prince. I like him, hut my mother and uncle do not favor the idea of my marrying a Persian, and so we wait. His name is Alirza Bizu Khan, and he is aide-de-camp to the Shah."

!! Many friends will be interested to hear of the marriage at Capetown last month ot Mr. J. Hubert Davies, of Johannesburg. to Miss C. Marie L. Jaski- Cant, of Haarlem, Ho! and. Mr. Davies, who is the eldest Ron of the late Rev. J. D. Davies, a Congrega- tional minister of Wareham. Dorsat, was for some years chief draughtSman to Woodhouse and Rawson, but four years ago went out to Johannes i burg,w here he has rapidly acquired a leading position n his profession, being now consulting electrical engineer to the Johannesburg Lighting Company and the Pretoria Lighting Company,besides enjoying a large practice with the mining companies of the Trans- vaal. He is in his thirty-fourth year. Mrs. Davies is the only daughter of the late Mr. R. Jaski-Carst, and niece of Captain A.G. Miirser Bruyns, .K.N.S.M., ! being thus connected with some of the best families in Holland.

Prebendary Whittington, who was preaching in .'t• Paul's Cathedral on Sunday morning, bears a name which seems of necessity to associate him with the sGund uf Bow Bells. In virtue of his emin- ent position ih the ecclesiastical world of London, he has a good deal to do with the fortunes of the city churches,on whose historic interests and archi- tectural beauties he is an authoritative and genial gossip. Amongst the livery companies with which he has been officially connected is the Merchant Taylors, in whose spacious half it recently fell to his lot to present its freedom to the Duke of York. lie is better known than any cleric of his day to the clerks aud apprentices of London, because of his long and enthusiastic exertions on behalf of the city of London College, whose principalship he has , held from the beginning.

: Dr. Symes Thompson, who is lecturing this week at the Gresham College on "The nose and the , mouth," is a thoroughly Cockney medico. With , his fresh, robust face and his stalwart frame, he does not look his fifty-five years; but for many Londoners it will be hard to realise that he has been responsible for the Chair of Physic in Basinghall- str.et. ever since he was thirty years old. He has , en hereditary right to speak upon all matters re- lating to the throat, for his father, the physician to the Brompton Ifo,pital, produced a work entitled the " Annals of influenza," which has been of im- mense service to medical science, and has provided a foundation for the historic study of an epidemic disease which has been again ravaging Europe for the last three years. Dr. Thompson is an old ! Pauline, obtained his professional training at King's ! College, has achieved the position at Brompton formerly held by his father, and as president of the Medical Society of London has attained one of the chief prizes open to the faculty.

Dr. Alexander 31acalister,F.R.S.,who was talking about Egyptian mummies at the Anthropological Institute on Tuesday evening, is, on the other hand a clever and typical Irishman. He has not yet at- tained his half century, but his reputation as an anatomist is so great that ten years ago he was eagerly snatched by Cambridge University from his native city of Dublin, where he had obtained his education, and at who-e university he held biologi- cal professorships for nearly 15 years. Ile still re- tains his membership of the Senatus of the Irish University, and is authority on current problems of scientific education. When at Dublin he wrote a couple of primers on animal morphology, which at once became recognised as text-books formally or informally in every medical school in the coun- try, end it was the impressive accuracy and lucidity ot these introductions to his subject which proved his mastery of it, and led a few years later to his Cambridge appointment.

Alphonse De Candolle, who has just died at Geneva, belonged to ft family which has occupied the same supremacy in the French botanical world during the century that has been occupied in this country by. the Hookers. Just as Sir William Hooker was succeeded at Kew-gardens by his son, Sir Joseph Hooker; so Augustin De Candolle was suc- ceeded in the Botanic Garden at Geneva by his son Alphonse. Further than this, the monumental his- tory of plants prepared by Sir Joseph under the designation "Genera Plantarum" ranks as a botani- cal classic, side by side with De Candolle's "Prodro- mus," each work having taken about thirty years to compile. The French professor also issued an introduction to botanical study which may be put side by side with Hooker's "Students' Flora." De Candolle received at various times all the honours usually bestowed upon foreign savants in this coun- try. He held honorary degrees from the universi- ties of Oxford and Cambridge, and received the Linnean gold medal four years ago. Nis work will in all probability be continued by his son Casimir, who is himself fifty-five years old, and for many veers has assisted his father in the preparation of his immense herbarium.

The Mr. Fitzgerald, whose letter to a Tory peer was quoted with such striking effect by Mr. Glad- stone in moving the second reading of the Home Rule Bill, turns out to be the Hon. James Edward- Fitzgerald, Auditor-General of New Zealand. Mr. Gladstone prefaced his reading of the letter by stating that he and Mr. Fitzgerald were friends 50 years ago, before the latter emigrated to New Zea- land. No doubt this is a reference to the days when Mr. Gladstone was a hard and indefatigable student at the British Museum, for Mr. Fitzgerald was an assistant in the National Library from 1844 to 1848, and held the post or under-secretary in the Museum from 1848 to 1850, when he resigned and accompanied the first batch of " Canterbury Pil- grims" (pioneer colonists sent out under the auspices of the Church of England) to New Zealand. Immediately after landing there he founded and edited the" Lyttleton Times ," became first super- intendent of the Province of Canterbury, was re- turned to the first New Zealand Parliament, and achieved a high reputation there as an orator and

statesman. Although now 76 years of age, he con- tinues to hold the important office of Auditor- General of the colony. He graduated at Cambridge in 1842. His wife is a daughter of the late George Draper, of London.

Apropos of the Queen's autographs which are being offered for sale at Sotheby's, it is not gener- ally known that her Majesty has taken of late to use an automatic pencil of the form which drops into position on being pressed at the top, and falls back into its protecting tube on being reversed. The Queen is very conservative in her habits so far as official penmanship is concerned, many descrip- tions of State papers, which require her signature only, being signed with a quill. But for letters 1 which are written throughout with her own hand she will frequently use a steel pen. In every room ever used by her Majesty, an inkstand is always kept, there being one, in ivory and ebony, even in oak dining room at Windsor Castle, an apartment which one would imagine to be required for prandial purposes only. At any moment, however, on all sorts of unexpected occasions, a signature or a private note may have to be written, and prepara- tion is made for this contingency accordingly.

Mr DEAR NELLIE,

Up to the present I have seen nothing specially i new n the fashion of capes. They continue getting shorter in the skirt, and are now not worn below the hips. They are being made, as before, chiefly in faced sloth, but almost any material is permis- sible, and as the weather gets warmer the very newest capes will be composed of black satin. This is a delightful material, and vastly becoming to young and old alike. It is quite vain for us to grumble at the monotony and long-continued popularity of these capes. Popular they are, and popular they will remain until we agree to curtail in some measure the enormous dimensions of our sleeves and revers. With such sleeves as are now being turned out by the leading dressmakers, the narrow-sleeved jacket of last year becomes an utter impossibility. But we must have jackets, if it's only for the sake of th it variety which is so charm- ing to ihe heart of every woman as regards her wardrobe. In some eases the coats are lined throughout with a bright-coloured silk to match the lining of the cape, but as often as not it is only the cape that Ls so adorned, and the inside lining is of a snore sombre hue, The coloured velvet capes of which we bear so much will never become common, partly on account of their costliness and delicacy. A very taking bonnet is one known by the name of "The Empire." It is made of fancy straw trimmed with velvet ribbon with an ostrich feather falling over the front. At the back of the feather is an upright loop of velvet and an aige-ette, and ribbon strings should be added to tie losely under the chin.

Several skirts are being made trimmed with flounces ; a very pretty one, eminently suitable for a young girl, was flounced to the waist with wilite satin, alternating with bands of lace insertion. Plain serge walking costumes are frequently adorned with little groups of flounces, three tiny frills being set round the feet, three more round the knees, and three again round the hips. It is, however, only a tall woman who can carry this off successfully, for it has rather 0 tendency to shorten, as indeed have all trimmed skirts, but on the whole I do not find those which extend round the hem alone possess that drawback; I have seen short women looking remarkably well In them. _ .

Avery pretty walking costume is one made of fancy cloth and trimmed with velvet and narrow ruche. The skirt is of the rather plain shape now so fashionable, and the lining and material are made up together. The lining of the bodice is tight fitting and covered with material. It fits the shoulder, and the back and front of the waist is drawn by small pleats. The sleeves are of the "leg of mutton" shape, and are made over a fitted lining foundation. The cape is made with a pointed yoke, which is twice pleated at back and front to make the fall. The whifte is finished with a band collar edged with ruche.

In Paris they are wearing shiny sailor hats of black American cloth, and are decking them with flower posies, which give them an altogether incon- gruous appearance, and one I sincerely hope our milliners will not attempt to imitate. The newest kind of artificial flowers for the moment are frosted violets, of exquisite appearance, which are being used with good effect on all kinds of millinery. The new materials for spriok wear are most fascinating. Deapcst violet, palest mauve, helio- trope shot with ripples of purple, and every con- ceivable combination of all four, appear to be the leading colours. Although the weaving of such wonders must remain a mystery, the subtle effects which result are very charming. As a mark of the advent of spring primroses are almost with us, at any rate with such of us as have to depend upon Nature for our supply. I was told the other day that for those who suffer from excitable, over-wronght nerves nothing is more calming than two or three handfuls of primroses thrown into the water of a bath. Although It seems almost cruel to put such delioate flowers to such prosaio a purpose, nerves are so much trouble to one in these days of too much to do that any relief is eagerly grasped at.

Amongst the spring dresses for children, the prettiest I have seen is one made of two materials, the lower portion being serge or cloth, and the upper portion of spotted or plaid material. The dress should he made with a lining yoke and the full front and back are gathered. In the centre the superfluous fulness is arranged in a double box pleat which is joined to the yoke and hidden with a piece of ribbon or velvet. 1 Yours very iincerely, JENNix.

Walking, the best of all exercises for the well, because I the most natural, should be a duty, just as are eating, drinking, or sleeping ; and the cares and supposed duties of the home should be laid aside until it has been enjoyed. It is a little difficult to determine just when custard is sufficiently cooked. This will settle the point : Take a spoonful of the mixture and let it run back into the dish; i it t leaves the spoon entirely, more cooking is necessary; should there be a little speck upon the spoon it is quite time to remove the " soft " custard from the range. -- A good rule for hanging pictures is to fix them with the centre of the picture at a level with the eye—i.e., about 51t. 3in. fmm thefloor. Supposing the pictures to be hung at this height, the superiority of the rail is noticeable over the old method of banging them to nails at about ceiling level; there is not such a display

of cord, and the pictures hang more securely.— " Work." on!,

" Not ly is it very healthy to yawn," says a French physician, " but artificial yawning should be resorted to in cases ef sore throat, buzzing of the ears, catarrh, and like troubles." It is said to be as efficacious in its way as gargling thethroat, with which process it should be combined. The reason stated is that during the act of yawning there is considerable stretching of the muscles of the pharynx and soft palate, which are in this way put through a sort of massage ; besides this, in the act of yawning the throat tubes contract and drive into the pharynx the mucus that has accumulated.

Apricot Sponge Cake. Cut a sponge cake into horizontal slices. Boil up for fifteen minutes a sufficient quantity of apricots and juice (say a pint for six penny sponge cakes) with sugar, an ounce (or two, if liked sweet) to each pint. Spread each slice with the apricots, and put them one on the top of the other, baste with the juice.; then pour over all a pint of thick custard, or whipped cream. The apricot pulp boiled up with sugar makes a nice dressing for this cake. " Tinned Meats, Fish, Fruits, and flow to Use Them."

FOX HUNTING IN FRANCE.

Of English sports introduced during the past few years into France not the least popular is that of fox hunting ; and a correspondent sends to the " Daily Graphic" some amusing particulars of a meet at a provincial French town near the Swiss frontier. The " Chasse Jan Renard," writes the correspondent has little in common with English fox hunting. French "chasseurs" seem to believe , the English custom of riding to hounds only in winter a senseless superstition, and hunt by prefer- ence in July and August. Some days previously to the meet a curious document, rather like a playbill, made its appearance. It contained an announce- ment that a meet would be held, and that there I would be 50 dogs (the French have no distinctive ' term for hounds) with whippers-in, " trompes," and " valets de chiens." The programme proceeded to say that at six there would be a " reveil en fanfare," and at half-past eight was the time announced for the meet, or rather the second meet, for at seven o'clock there was to be a preliminary "reunion at the Casino. Punctually at six on the day appointed the town was awakened by the " reveil en fanfare." In the music there was but little suggestion of hunting, and in the equipment of the band, for a band it was, there was if possible still less. Four " trompes," arrayed in uniform of scarlet and black with three-cornered hats, each blowing lustily into a huge " cor de chasse," were dragged aimlessly about the town in a local hackney car- riage. The whippers-in, though more suggestive of hunting, were of a type unknown on this side of the Channel. They were a sylvan costume of green and buff, doubtless not unlike that worn by the followers of Robin Hood. Why they carried guns is a mystery. The , dogs, of no particular breed, were coupled ; and they, at I least' seemed to have no ambition to hunt. The sport I began to the moment of the advertised time. No time was lost in starting a fox. The huntsmen had with great fnrothought brought two in a sack for the use of subscribers. The first of these animals seemed to have a fair chance of escape, for it was some minutes before the hounds could be induced to follow. Even then there was an evident lack of enthusiasm. But the fox evi- dently did not realise that he was to be hunted, and failed to make use of his opportunity. After a short run he was overtaken by the one hound who seemed to take an interest in the proceedings. The second fox was squally unfortunate ; and now the defects of the French system of not finding foxes became apparent, for the hunt came to a premature end, the idea of starting a fresh fox seeming to strike no one. But perhaps foxes are scarce in France. The return of the hunting party to town was a kind of triumphal entry. The foxes were slung on a pole borne by the huntsmen in their sylvan costumes much in the same manner as the dead deer is carried in the woodland scene of " As You Like It." But the merry chorus of the banished huntsmen," What shall he have who killed the deer 2" was replaced by a hideous bellowing of the " cors de chasse." A charac- teristic feature of the French hunt was the doctor who followed with a little cart bearing the legend, " Secours aux blesses." Happily his services were not required.

A BOSTON GIRL'S DESCRIPTION OF FOOITALL.

A Boston girl is supposed to have written this graphic and illuminating description of football, as seen by feminine eyes : First came some men all dressed in blue, Then others came in red, One fellow yelled, the rest all tried To jump upon his head. And then another fellow yelled, And each man, where he stood, Just hit, and struck, and knocked and kicked, At every one he could. And then one fell upon his neck, And all the otheoi ran, And on his prone and prostrate form Leaped every blessed man. And then the ambulance drove on, And, loading up with men With twisted necks and broken lungs, Went driving off again.

EXCITING SCENE AT A FOOTBALL

MATCH.

During the progress of the match between St. Helens and Salford at Salford, on Saturday, the Sal- ford supporters showed great antipathy to Sudlow, the well-known three-quarter of St. Helens, who formerly played for Salford. Sudlow is a St. Helens man, but some time ago he was engaged at Salford, and joined the club there. On his return at the commence- ment of this season, he got his transfer to the St. Helens club, and has since played regularly with them. On Saturday he was booed and booted, and the police had to guard him from the threatened onslaughts of the spectators, and while going with the teams after the match to headquarters in a waggonette half a dozen policemen and a number of detectives acted as escorts. At the headquarters of the Salford club a large number of persons assembled, and Sudlow had to be taken in a round-about way in a cab to Manchester, where he left for St. Helens. The officials of the St. Helens club co n- sider the affair a great insult to their club, and intend to bring the matter before the County Committee.

PRIZEFIGHT AT ST. HELENS.

Early on Sunday morning a prize fight for stakes of .£lO took place in a secluded spot at Eccleston, outside the borough of St. Helens. At seven o'clock a large number of sporting men left St. Helens, and on arriving at the plaee mentioned a ring was formed, and the two combatants, named Ball and Kelly, both working men, who are well known members of the local fraternity, faced each other. The fighting was very keen at the commencement, and there was little difference between the two. Ball appeared to be the favourite. though he received the worst punishment. Twenty three rounds were fought, and the time was one hour and twenty minutes, when Kelly's seconds threw up the sponge, and Ball was declared the victor. Both men were terribly punished.

LICENSING PROSECUTION AT RUNCORN.

CONFLICTING EVIDENCE. At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, before Dr. Steele and Mr. P. Speakman, Thomas Davies, land- lord of the Mersey Vaults, Mersey-et., was summon- ed for permitting drunkenness to take place on his premises en the 28th March, and Bridget Rawlinson and Rose Ann Corcoran, both married women of Sal keld- square, was charged with being drunk on the premises. 1 Mr. A. Fletcher, of Northwich, prosecuted for the police and Mr. J. Burton defended. P.S. Gosling stated that ' on the night of the 28th March, he was on duty in Mer- sey-st. At ten o'clock he visited the Mersey Vaults,l because of the noise he heard proceeding thence, and in I a small compartment he found two men and three women. Twe of the latter Bridget Rawlinson and Rose Ann Corcoran had a glass ef,porter each, and they were I both drunk. The barman fetched the landlord, who on being informed of the sondition of the women, ieniea that they were drunk, and sai4 they had been washing there until half-past seven. The sergeant remarked that ' they had had plenty of time to get drunk since then and 1 Rawlinson commenced to scream and use abuse. As the officer was going out, the landlord made use of some strong expressions, characterizing the constable's con- duct in entering the house as insolence. The sergeant replied that he was doing his duty in pointing out defects, in the manner in which the house was carried out, and he told the landlord that he had had too much drink and that he was not a proper person to conduct a licensed house. There was no doubt the landlord was under the influence of drink. Afterwards Rawlinson created a disturbance in the street, and eventually he was compelled to lock her up. At half-past eleven Davies came to the police station accompanied by Dr. MacLen- nan, and said he wanted to see Rawlinson, and the officer told him that he had no authority to bring the woman from the cells,and that unless the woman herself made an application neither Davies nor the doctor could see her. She had made no such application.—Evidence was given by Inspector Cooper, P.C. Beckett, and P C. Plevin that Rawlinson was very drunk, and that she made the cell smell of liquor.—The defendant sworn, said that on the night in question the house was as quiet as the court at that momext, and he contended that the women were not drunk, and could walk straight. During the conver- sation with the police, P.S. Gosling told him that he would be better if he did not take quite as many glasses, and he was annoyed at that remark, and replied that "it was just like his— impudence to say so."—By Mr. Fletcher : To his knowledge Mrs. Rawlinson was never served during the day. When she was washing, after she had finished she went into the bar and had been there three quarters of an hour when the police en- tered.—Sabrina Davies, wife of last witness, gave Raw- linson a good character as a washer-woman, and said she had nothing to drink during the day. Both women were sober when the police entered. They had been in the drinking part of the house for an hour and a half when the police arrived.—James Wilmot, barman, said Mrs. Rawlinson had only two small bottles of stout and was sober, quiet, and orderly.— Richard Smith, checker on the Ship Canal, and John Kerr, agent for a photographic firm, and Annie Jolley, servant at the vaults, gave evidence that the women were sober.—Mrs. Rawlinson said she had only two small bottles of stout, and she alleged that in the street Sergeant Gosling pushed her, and had she been drunk she would have had an awful fall.—Mrs. Corcoran said one small bottle of porter was all she had during the day. —The magistrates, after a brief consultation in private, dismissed the whole of the summonses.

PARLIAMENTARY GOLF TOURNAMENT.

LOCAL MEMBERS DISTINGUISH THEMSELVES. The play in the Parliamentary Golf Tournament was interrupted by the Easter holidays, and until Thursday very little had been done to get through the second ties. On Thursday forenoon, however, there were a good many players at Furzedown, and among the matches decided were two in which Lancashire members achieved notable successes. In the early part of the day Mr. Seton-Karr met Mr. Felix Skene, one of the steadiest golfers in the tournament, who has hitherto always got well within sight of the final tie. The member for St. Helens received three strokes from his opponent, and he made the best use of this handicap by playing a strong game. Mr. Skene was a little out of form, but even if he had played his best he would have found it difficult to pull off the game. As it was, Mr. Seton-Karr won by three up and two to play. In the afternoon the Hon. T. W. Legit played off his tie with Mr. John Penn, who is the scratch man of the handicap. Mr.Legh was allowed seven strokes in the round, and with these odds he won a smart victory by two holes. The member for the New- ton Division was, like Mr. Seton-Karr, in his best form. He drove steadily, and used his iron and -fitter with excellent effect. Mr. Penn played up exceptionally well at the end of the match, doing the last five holes in fifteen.; but by that time Mr. Legh had the match well in hand, and making no mistakes, in spite of the bril- liant play of his opponent, he finished the match at the seventeenth hole.

THE POLLUTION OF THE SHIP CANAL DISCUSSION IN THE MANCHESTER CITY COUNCIL.

At the meeting of the Manchester Corporation on Wednesday, upon the motion for the approval of the proceedings of the Rivers Committee, Sir J. Harwood said he was asked on the previous day, at a meeting of the Ship Canal Committee, when the sewage works were likely to be opened. It was very important in the interests of the Ship Canal that the sewage should be turned into the sewage works as soon as possible, because at the present time, in consequence of the short- ness of water, the canal was emitting a very bad smell indeed. (Hear, hear.) It showed that there was a good deal of bad matter going down the canal. It would enable Manchester to ask other people to do something if they could show a good example themselves. If the chairman could give them an assurance when the sewage works would be opened and the sewage diverted it would be a very great advantage to the canal and a blessing to the city.—Mr. Alderman Thompson, in reply, said he could give no precise date as to when the sewage works would be opened. He expected the date would be about October. He hoped the work would be pushed on, and that it might be completed before that time.

A LADY LITIGANT; • CROSS-EXAMINING A HUSBAND. Many successful novelists will wish they could have invented the scene which actually occurred in the Divorce Court on Wednesday, when Mrs. M'Kerrow, who is petitioning for a divorce, had the pleasure of putting her husband through a severe cross-examination. In one of his answers, the respondent said that his wife " had a pistol in her jacket pocket" when she called upon him on one occasion ; whereupon the lady replied, "1 should not have much regard for my figure if I carried pistols in my pocket." The petitioner appears to have made herself thoroughly acquainted with her hus- band's career. She challenged him with having forged a marriage certificate with which he had " deceived another girl," and then she produced letters which he had written to Miss Shepherd (one of the four "inter- veners ") and put some scathing questions to him on their contents. The respondent admitted that ha was " heartily ashamed ' of the letters, and that his mind must have been affected. " The injury to my spine," he said, " affected my head and my memory." "Yes," ;interjected the pitiless cross-examiner, but it did not affect your heart." The entertainment was suspended by the adjournment of the court.

DARKWOOD.

A TALE OF LONEMOOR. BY MRS. HAP.ILIETT LEWIS, Author of "The Sundered Hearts," "The Bailiff" Scheme," "The Double Life," &a

CHAPTER XXIV. TwO ADVERTISEMENTS.

Miss Georgina Tollish was a girl of fifteen, short of stature and stout of frame, built up upon the obese pattern of her father. See had no great depth of mind, and was distinguished for her perfect good nature. She was silly and shallow, self conscious and self-assertive—in short, a loud-tongued, shrill- voiced, bouncing creature, whom her father regard- ed i'vith an absolute abhorrence. Yet, such as she was, she was Lord Darkwood's heiress, and as the Darkwood title, like certain others, descended in the female line, in default of male heirs, she might be Marchioness of Darkwood in her own right after her father's death.

As she burst into his presence like a small incar- nate whirlwind, the marquis started back in anger and dismay; but the girl ran forward and threw her arms around his ne3h, giving him a loud-sound- ing kiss. He pushed her from him with a look of disgust.

" Where did you come from Georgina ?" he exclaimed aogrily. "How did you find me? How dared you come to Eugland without my permis- sion ?"

The girl bestowed a prolonged stare around her before she cried out admiringly— .

" i'sfy I ain't this jolly? Ain't this style? Ain't it per tectly scrumptious ?" She stooped so that her short, shabby dress lay upon the floor, and made a prancing movement backwards and forwards, regarding attentively her reflection in an opposite mirror. "Georgina," cried her father, "answer my ques- tion, miss. Do you bear me ?" Miss Georgina looked up unconcernedly. Evi- dently she was used to harshness, and not at all sensitive to it.

"Why, yes, I heard you," she answered. "I'm not deaf. How did I find you?' Why, I saw in Galignani's Messenger that the Marquis of Dark- wood was dead, and that Captain Fabian Tollish had succeeded to the title and estates. That's how I knew where you were. Where I came from you know as well as I do."

the marquis frowned angrily. "How dared you come to England without my permission," he again .demanded.

4 "I dare 'do a great deal," replied the girl, coolly. "I haven't been brought up to care much, you know. If I had waited for you to send for me I should never have come. You never acted much like a father to me, anyhow. But if you're a lord, and living in clover, I'm a lord's daughter, and I won't live in poverty. I told the people there all my grandeurs that I intended to have, you know, and f brought the old chambermaid, Guiseppina, with me for a maid in style, you see. She's in the servants' hall, I suppose." Georgina.'s perfect coolness and unconcern served to inflame her father's anger. She tore oil her bat and tocsed it upon the sofa, and threw her sacque upon another. Her hair was frowsy ; her garments were ill-fitting and thread. bare ; her boots were broken at the sides, and her gloves were:. ere rags. . . . .

" You look like aTheggar, Georgina," ejaculated Lord Da.rkwood. "You're a pretty figure to come iuo a room like this."

" Whose fault is it ?" retorted the daughter. " wear better clothes if I had them."

The marquis winced under this reproach. His life had l3;en one of supreme selfishness. He had married the daughter of a fellow army officer, had dissipated his wife's fortune, and after her death had thrown off all responsibility in regard to his child. She had grown up in a cheap boarding- house and a cheap school alternately. He considered that in paying her bills he did his entire duty by her, and that in making those bills as small as possible he was doing his duty by himself. Had the child been pretty, or remarkably intelligent, he woud no doubt have treated her differently ; but he disliked her and was ashamed of her. He had never bestowed a thought upon her future or her settlement in life, never considered that there must come a time when cheap schools and cheap board- ing houses would cease to be proper homes for her. i‘.nd now here she was upon his hands, a great, coarse, overgrown girl, whose very appearance he conceived to be a disgrace to him. . .

" You'll have to g-o back with Guiseppina," he exclaimed. " The fly in which you came from the station is gone. You can stay till morning, but you must go then." "Not if I know it ! How would it look for Lord Darkwood's daughter to live in a boarding-house garret, as I've been doing, while Lord Darkwood himself lives in splendour? No, sir, I'm here, and I shall stop here. You'll have to put me out by force, and I warn you not to do that. I'd kick up such a rumpus," said this terrible young woman, "as to make the name of Darkwood a scandal and a byword to the country." Ihe marquis knew that she would keep her word. And what would the world say if it knew that Lord Darkwood had sent back his daughter and heiress to a second-class boarding-house in Valetta while he resided in luxury and splendour at Dun. holm Castle? Clearly, his design was not feasible. He must make other provision for her. . . . .

"You took me so completely by surprise in your coming, Georgina," he said, "that I scarcely knew what to say to you. Upon consideration, I will allow you to remain in England. Guiseppina must start for Malta to morrow morning. I suppose you know, Georgina," and his tone was more conciliating "that you are my heiress. If I should not marry again—which I shall do, I may as well tell you now —you would be Marchioness of Darkwood in your own right. There is, of course, a possibility that you may come into the title and inherit the estates, even if I do marry." " Well ?" said Miss Georgina, "You have been neglected, I own; but I must say in my own justification that I never expected to become Lord Darkwood. I expected to continue in the army as plain Captain Tollish, unless I should be obliged by my creditors to sell out, as seemed very likely. In such case, I should have made some provision for you. Now you must be eklacated, polished, transformed into a lady. You shall have the best instructors, the mom assiduous care," "mm," said the young lady, "we'll see about that. I've been my own mistress too long to sub. rnit to pastors and masters. I'll have fine clothes, I know that, and a maid to wait upon me, and a horse. If you're a lolcl, I'm a lady, whether I'm educated or not."

"A lady by courtesy," said the marquis; "but no mere title can make you a lady." " Well, hav'nt I a title?"

"Yes," replied Lord Darkwood, who had not be- fore given a thought to the subject. "My father was a Charteris, and took the name of his wife, who came of high family, and who brought hita a fortune. I have relinquished my mother's name, and taken the name to which my father was born. lam Fabian Charteris, Lord Darkwood. You are Lady Georgina Charteris." "A very pretty name," declared its owner. "I shall he mistress here until you bring home a new wife. I shan't go to any boarding school. I set my foot down flat as to that. I have had a hard time in my life so tar. I mean to have a good time henceforth.'

"What an obstinate, pigheaded creature," ex- claimed Lord Darkwood. "You'll have to obey me, Georgina. lam your master, as you will dis- cover, if you attempt to defy me." The girl giggled after a silly fashion. She had little awe of her father. She felt that he had neg. leetecl and wronged her, and she was determined to " stand up for her rights" thenceforth, and to tight for them if necessary. Yet 30 shallow was she that she even then, when her best interests were at stake, giggled over his look of anger and annoy- ance, and arranged the folds of her dress, and craned her head airily, viewing her reflection in the mirror. "I'll tell you what do," she remarked. " You can get me a governess. She must come here to live. No more school for me. I shall stay here at Dunhohn Castle, and I'll make a row if you attempt to send me away, BO that's settled l" It was settled. No argument could move the girl from her resolve. Her father's angry commands had no effect upon her. She was obstinate and pig- headed to the last degree. Threats and promises were alike without avail. She had made her deter- mination' and nothing could move her. Lord Darkwood found himself vanquished at last.

"You can remain," he said, sullenly. "I will pro- cure a governess for you. It is nearly dinner time, and you are not fit to appear at the table If you are to remain here, you must conduct your- self properly, and not excite the curiosity and gos- sip of the servants. Did you bring a box with you?" " Yes ; but this is the best I have. You were always stingy with me, you know," said her lady- ship. "You must stay in your room till you can have a decent outfit. Leave off your slang. Try and do me credit."

The marquis touched the bell pull. A servant, swift and noisele.s, a ppearsd• "My compliments to Mrs. Dover, said NG ship, politely, "and tell her that I requ,st be!, presence in the drawing-room for a few moments. The servant withdrew, and a liAle later the housekeeper came in. She was a gentlewoman, elderly, relined and in- telligent, the wife of a country rector. Sie ha.l be`,ll for many years an himuured in ma a of the hoase. The late Lord Darkwond had never brought limn° a wife, and his father had been many yeare widower, so that she had been virtually mistress 0 the castle. Her administrative abilities were of a high order ; she was a strict disciplinarian. as the servants could have testified, yet they tdlliked her. Under her rule, perfect peace and order reigned throughout the sumptuous dwelling. She was ie mourning for her late master, whom she had loVed with a mother's tenderness and devotion. She looked from the new lord, whom she instinct- ively disliked, to his daughter. " Mrs. Dover," said the marquis courteously, "I have told you that I am a widower. This is uif my daughter and only child—my heiress—LadY Georgina Charteris." Mrs. Dover bowed, drawing nearer the girl witll6' look of ir terest not unmixed with surprise. This girl Lord Darkwood's drughter ! Why there were housemaids in the castle who would surpass her io looks and breeding. " I am happy to welcome yen to Dunholm, Loa Georgina," she said in her gentle voice. " Well, I'm glad to get here," replied the girls quite boisterously. "I've come straight from Malts.. I've travelled day and night, and I m tired ahnosC to death." "My daughter's education has been sadly Deg; hotted, Mrs. Dover," said his lordship blandly. "I have trusted too much to teachers,who have proreu'' incompetent. My poor motherless girl requires 6 good deal of care and instruction, but we shall make amends for past neglect. I shall find a goree" ness for her as soon as possible. In the meantime, Mrs. Dover, please do what you can for her. I sr.„111 give you a cheque in the morning, and you vrl'h oblige me by taking the carriage and going ' Shrewsbury to order an outfit for her." Mrs. Dover bowed assent. "And now," concluded his lordship, "be Pea' enough to assign her suitable rooms and a maid 0, attend her. She will dine in her own room. ShT will remain in her apartments until the defects or, her wardrobe shall be remedied." Lady Georgina looked sullen, but acquiesced ' this arrangement, as appeared by her silence. "Will you accompany me to my room, Ladt Georgina ?" asked Mrs. Dover. "I shall have ycl,r ,rooms prepared as soon as possible,. but they 'Ad° require to be aired and warmed." dine with you, then, Mrs. Dover," said the Lady Georgina, "since lam not fit to dine with 019 father. lam hungry as a hunter, and the sooner I have dinner the better." Mrs. Dover withdrew with her charge, and the marquis muttered— " Fool that I am not to have sent a trustwortht agent a month ago to remove the girl to a Paris convent. She is here on my hands, a loutish, still creature that I hate the very sight of. I'll get governess for her. I'll send an advertisement the London newspapers to-morrow." Lady Georgina dined with Mrs. Dover in the housekeeper's private parlour. The elegance a this apartment surprised the girl. The Lady Georgina sat down by the bright hearth ' after dinner, and meditated upon the magnificence of her future toilets. She had just deci led in her own mind to order a bright blue ?noir, antiqn6 gown, to be trimmed with white lace, for 6111,1 wear, and a white hat to wear with it, when a mai' appeared, announcing to Mrs. Dover that the Ladi. Georgina's rooms were ready for occupancy. "Let me show you upstairs, my dear,' said the housekeeper kindly, feeling a pity for this neg!ectecl girl, to whom even nature had been-unkind and nig* gardly. "I hope you will like y0...r rooms. The have a pretty outlook." The girl regarded the lady superciliously. "Be kind enough to address me by my title," sh exclaimed. "I am the Lady Georgina CharterlSr' Mrs. Dover, if you please." A faint, amused smile crept about the lady's HOP; but she repressed it instantly. She conducted the girl up the great s'nireassi with its frequent, landings and many niches, adorns, with statuary, to a wide and stately upper hall, 0., which many doors upon either side opened. Passing these doors, and turning into a corridor at right angles with the hall, Mrs. Dover led the: way to a second hall parallel with the first. Opening a door at the left Mrs. Dover usher the lady Georgina into a beautiful boudoir, upholstered in blue and silver, with wide French windows opening upon balconies, an I overlookiog the park and the windings of the Dark river. A bright fire VMS burning in the grate,and flowerg crowded the vases on the mantelpieces, brackets, and console table. "This is delightful !" cried the girl, charm?' with the luxurious apartments. "This is my ting room, I suppose. Is that the bedroom ?" , She hurried into the adjoining room. The bea' room, dressing room and bath room were all alike: perfect in their fitting up, and perfect also in th taste that had directed their adorning. "I shall be happy as a lark here," declared thit Lady Georgina. I won't have a stuffy, stuPht Foverness t o rule over me. I'll tell my father in the morning. I intend to be my own mistress. "You are very young, Lady Georgina," said Moss Dover. "Young ladies always have instructors ha': til they come out into society." "I'll be an exception to the rule. If my father, wanted to governess me he should have begas earlier. He has let me run wild till now, and .1 won't be ruled, so there ! Let Guiseppina come ur and see my rooms. I a ant her to carry back a Ohl that will astonish my 'Valetta friends. Old Guiseppina was : summoned, and the giA ' exhibited her rooms, and talked grandly of ti 4. wardrobe she was to have. The old Maltese woman went away the nerit, morning on her return to Valetta. Directly after she had departed for Shrewsburit, Georgina burst into tbe library, where her father was seated penning an advertisement, and inforro • Ishn in forcible language that she would not haves" governess. Lord Da.rkwood regarded her angrily. " What does this mean ?" he cried. "If you re', fuse to have a gsverness I'll put you in a Freuo', convent, Georgina. You will find that I he authority over you." "I won't go to a convent 1" shrieked the girl. a passion. "And I won't have a governess. you'll find a girl not much older than I am, We'' has been educated, I'd have her for a companion but I won't be under any one." The marquis expostulated and threatened, be the girl stoed firm. "I tell you," she said, more calmly, " le/. from example, and if you'll find me a pleasant you°, lady that I'll like, who is not over twenty years clur and ss ho won't taut on airs to me, I'll try to ccy, her and learn of her ! but I won't have any one iv° assumes authority over me." In alluding to measures which Cir Insa.,;%V.)- int, tu+.4 to bring before the House o eeon; !,,Illtei '",(4sl3ealier 'aid one chief measure was the s as Dtieh; 'N'al nermission to treat habitual drunkards . 41%1;441 1 auffcring from a disease. They c prePosel t - them by sending them to institutions for true(

===== 0002601_18930415_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
land six months, and treating them for their disease, instead of allowing them to be sent to jail for 14 days', and other terms, of imprisonment. (ffear, heat.) Another measure was to get the age of boys to be dealt with by their society—raised from from 14 years to 16 years—(hear, hear)—and she thought they who knew how often they were debarred from interfering on behalf of many poor little urchins who were above 14 years would agree with them. Another measure was to interfere with the insurance of baby children. This present system she considered one of the most terrible classes of temptation which could be placed before people, who regarded the satiation of some personal desire above the value of a child's life. It was a common thing for such people to insure their six month's old babies for a sum of 305., and then by various means, ill-feeding and neglect, bring about the child's death in an apparently natural manner. The aboli- tion of this system had been objected to by members of Parliament and others on the ground that it encouraged thrift on the part of this working classes. She could tell them that the working-class looked upon it with as much horror as anyone else, and would only be too glad to have the systom as it was now, abolished. It was a decidedly pernicious and evil system. (Applause.) The speaker next alluded to baby farming and gave some startling accounts of facts which had come before her during her personal experience. There were hundreds and hundreds of babies who were born contrary to a moral law, and yet who had to live because of illegal law, and which were placed in these farms and nothina more heard of them—nothing of the gradual pinings and miseries of their subsequent deaths. The terms upon which most of the children were taken into those farms upon, was the payment of £2O down for its keep and another £2O at its death. On the proposition of Mr. John White a vote of thanks I was passed to Miss Bolton, and on the proposition of Captain Rodgers a vote of thanks was also passed to the Chairman.

MY SKOOMESTER I

Wo wur that joker, grim an' gruff, Wo ne'er know'd wen he'd had enuff 0' oather whisky punch or snuff? My Skoomester !

Wo towt uz aw eaur A B C, With th' butt eend o' a within tree, An' never leet eaur breeches be ! My Skoomester !

Who eddicated uz to spell, Wi' causin' us to bawk an' yell, Wen warmin' wheer we dossn't tell— My Skeomester ! Wo, wi' that same owd within stick, Wud gie uz mony a ugly flick, O'er larnin' eaur arethmetick ! My Skoomester! Wo show'd uz heaw to do Cube Root, Wi polishin' his heyvy boot On th' back part o' my Sunday suit! My Skoomester! Oe'r grammar th' Owd Lad never miss'd To gie eaur ears a gradely twist ; Er gen uz " wc t for I" wi' his fist— My Skoomester! Pth' Bible turn, if aw wur dull, He'd gie his lungus arms a pull, An' play drum solos on my skull I My Skoomester! If e'er aw happen't to be late, By Godlins, lads! aw know'S my tate= His stick an' fist wad get agate ! My Skoomester! But welly fifty yers hey gone, Sin' aw wur so much put upon, By that mad castigatin' mon, My Skoomester 1 Wy, even neaw, they're -very fond 0' hellish canin' a lad's hand; To try an' make him understond Like my Skoomester! Well, peace be wi' him 1 but aw fear, Owd Nick hez getten him by th' ear, An' makes him pay fer punchin's dear— My Sko3mester ! TUM 0' PEGS.

Rose Bonk, 14, 4, 93.

SHOCKING WIFE ASSAULT AT WAR-

RINGTON.

A DRUNKEN HUSBAND'S BRUTALITIES.

Patrick Donohue was charged at Warrington Police Court, on Wednesday, with being drunk on Tuesday afternoon, in James-st. and also with assaulting his wife Mary Ann Donohue on Saturday and Tuesday. P.S. Taylor said that owing to a complaint from a boy, the Chief Constable sent hire to James-st.,on Tuesday, where prisoner and his wife lived. When he got there, he saw the wife with bruises the whole length of her arm, and holding to a chair. The husband who was also present was using very bad lan- guage te his wife, and was the gorse for drink. Witness brought him to the police station. P.C. Delahay cor- roborated. Mary Ann Donohue said that her husband had been drunk the greater part of Saturday night, and she had to stop with him to prevent him from falling into the fire. At about ten o'clock she went out for some meat, and when she returned her husband had gone to bed with his clothni and clogson. When she went upstairs, he jumped out of bed, and kicked her about her body, thrashed her with the buckle end of his belt, threw her downstairs, and pitched her into the street. On Tuesday he also struck her in the face. Mary Ann Rabbit said that with Dr. Peacocke, she examined Mrs. Donohue, and found four pieces taken out of her arm. The sides of her body were covered with bruises, and also her back. Catherine and Annie Cunningham each gave evidence of hearing screams coming out of prisoner's house on Friday night. The prisoner had a very bad record against him and the magistrates sent him to goal for six months, and also granted a separa- tion order, ordering him to pay 15s. a week to his wife, on coining out of prison, and giving the wile the charge of her four children.

WARRINGTON COUNTY COURT.

At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Messrs. Howarth and Yarwood, hay merchants, Weaver- ham, sued Robert Wilkinson, farmer, Whitley, for .£8 16s. 2d.. being the amount overpaid by them to him for a quantity of hay.—Mr. Burton, of Runcorn, appeared for plaintiffs, and stated that they bought from the defendant a stack of hay, the terms to be £3 I per ton if it exceeded 17 tons 10 cwt., and £2 15s. if it did not exceed that weight; £52 10s. was paid on ac- count, and the hay was afterwards cut and sent to Pres- ton Brook railway station to the plaintiffs' order, where it was weighted on the railway company's machine. Defendant only delivered ten loads, but the railway company's clerk had given eleven weigh tickets, thus giving one ticket twice oven—The railway porter was called and corroborated Mr. Burton's statement, and stated that the reason why defendant brought one load and then said that he might as well have all the tickets at the end. Be gave him the first ticket at that time, and afterwards put the same weight on another ticket. —Mr. A. Browne, who appeared for the defence, said his client declared that he had delivered eleven loads, but his BOTIOUr intimateu 4hot unleso Weft/ bcratimi .upport defendant's statement, he should take the version of the railway porter to be correct, and he therefore gave a verdict for plaintiffs.

MANTLES MADE MADE FOR ONE PENNY.

AD EVEN THAT DISPUTED. A claim of a most extraordinary character has been the subject of magisterial proceedings at the Guildhall P bee Court. Henry Myers, a mantlemaker, was sum- moned at the instance of Mary Ann Ellis for the payment of £1 4s. 10d.," for making 127 mantles, due March 25, 1893." The claim ran thus : "Week ending, March 25, forty-six mantles at id., 3s. 10.1. ; forty-four at 3d., lie.; sixteen at 30., 4s. Bd. ; six at 4d.2. ; six at 6d., 3s. ; brought forward, 4d.; total £1 4s. 100." The defendant admitted the debt, but claimed a reduction on the ground that the complainant had taken away some cloth. Complainant admitted that she had taken some " rags " (cuttings). These, however, were considered perquisites.—Defendant called his overseer, who said Ellis took away some cloth, but he could not say if she removed the same from the warehouse.— Mr. ' Alderman Treloar :Is it true that mantles were made for id ? —Complainant : Yes ; the whole of the sewing was done for a penny, and that, too, of fashionable mantles sold for £s.—An order was made at once for the claim, with costs, £1 los. lop. The decision was received with applause.

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY. APRIL 15. 1893. AT OUR LOCAL CLIURCHES.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE. The P.S.A. meeting held in Wycliffe Church, on Sun- day afternoon, was well attended. The chair was oc- cupied by Mr. Kerry, who delivered a brbf address. Mrs. S. W. Jeffery and Miss N. Roberts contributed solos in an excellent manner. Mr. Boreland, Liverpool, in the course of an address dwelt on the evils of indulging in intoxicating drink and gave some distressing inci- dents which had come under his own personal notice in Liverpool. Twenty-one new members were enrolled, making a total of 459. Several persons signed the pledge. THE CONGREGATIONAL MARTYRS OF 300 YEARS AGO.—SERMON BY THE REV. J. YONGE.

The Rev. John Yonge preached a special sermon in Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening, dealing with the celebration of the Congregational martyrs of 300 years ago, Barrowe, Greenwood and Penry, who were executed for their Congregational principles in April and May, 1593. There was a large attendance. Mr. Yonga took his text from Acts IV. part of 19 verse, and chapter 5, part of 29 verse. "Whether it be right in the sight of God to hearken unto you more than unto God,judge ye." " We ought to obey God rather than men." The rev. gentleman said these were apostolic utterances, and should therefore entirely commend themselves not only' to their acceptance, but also to the acceptance of those who claim exclusive apostolic authority for their church and their creed. They were also Nonconformist utter- ances, wherefore Nonconformity to unrighteous enact- ments whether of church, or state, or both, and refusal to conform conscience to mere human authority was apostolical. It surely must appear strange that churches loudly as- serting their apostolical origin and succession should, in course of their history, have so stubbornly constituted Nonconformity to themselves, both a sin against God, and a crime against society. They were reminded by the martyrdoms of 300 year ag,), which they commemor- ated that day, that such had been the case. In these days physical martyrdoms of prison, gibbet, and stake were no longer possible, yet, still, they had to face the indisputable fact that in many places and ways, even in this country persons whose consciences preclude them from conforming to the worship and creeds of the state church were subjected to many social and personal dis- abilities and disadvantages. Even at this very day,i Nonconformity is proclaimed from Episcopal pulpits as an " awful error " and a guilty " schism," and such as persist in their Nonconformity are regarded as only shielded from eternal catastrophe by the uncovenanted mercies of God. It has neen properly held most desir- able that our Congregational people in all parts of the world should be invited to ce'ebrate the 300th annivers iry of the three principal confessors of our Congregational faith. Not alone that the men who as nobly suffered, I deserve to be held in everlasting remembrance ; but be- cause it is much more desirable that our people ot to- day, specially our younger people, should be reminded of what it cost our Congregational ancestors to secure this religious freedom, which we, their posterity enjoy. This ter-centenary of the martyrdom of Barrowe, Green- wood and Penry is an occasion therefore that may serve a useful purpose, if it succeed in rendering newly con- spicuous the meaning of Congregational Nonconf • rmity, and the holy baptism of noble Christian blood which consecrated its earliest years. It has been alleged by the Rector of Warrington that they were not martyrs for their opinions, but that they were executed for criminal offences against the peace and safety of the kingdom. Speaking of Robert Browne, who about 1570 was the first to bring the Congregational view into prominence in this country, Mr. Willis, stated as a result of his teaching " plots of all kinds were incited against Queen Eliz ibetti, and five independents were hanged. Two men (and Mr. Willis should have said that these had been clergy in his own church), Thacker and CApping were hanged in 1583, and three othere, Barrowe Greenwood, and Penry were hanged in 1593 ; but" added Mr. Willis "I want you ..o note that they were punished by the State, not by the church: they were punished by the State for seditious language against the Queen." The Rector is simply there declaring the Anglican position upon this, to them, very awkward and embarrassing event in English his- tory. He does not bring forward any evidence what- ever, in support of his allegation. Being himself, as he most frankly states. thankful for the doctrine of church authority, he wishes his people to accept English history as he, their priest and pastor may give it them. They (the Congregationalists) however, who don't owe any ecclesiastical allegiance to the Rector, may respectfully decline to accept as accurate, even from him, that which is not historical. History candidly read, makes it clear thaz these three men were not, as the Rector alleges, traitors to the Queen and country, but martyrs for their religious beliefs, instrumentally sacrificed, no doubt, by the hand of the civil power, but really slain at the will and instigation of thedominant church. Now rightly to grasp the position of 300 years ago, the date of these executions, consider what led up to it. It was just at the point in English history, when the combined power in matters in religion of Priest and Prince had become intolerable, that our spiritual ancestors, longing for religious liberity, fell back on the principles of Congrega- tionalism. 'Tnose principles admit in matters religious, the intervention of no priest, and refuse the intervention of any prince. This was the time of the re-discovery of Congregationalism. Congregationalism itself is as old as Christianity. But speaking now of Congregationalism in England, it undoubtedly sprang, at this period, from Puritanism. The Puritan party arose in the days of Edward VI within the established church, and was com- posed of men longing for simplicity of faith and ritual, and strongly protesting against the Romish doctrines and ceremonies which yet infested the Reformed Church. On June 19, 1567, a company of Puritans assembled in Plummer's Hall, London, and were broken in upon by the authorities and the leaders sent to prison. This company had been already constituted a Congregational Church, and was the first in this country of which there is any record. In 1571, a Puritan clergyman, n-imed Robert Brown, succeeded, however, in collecting several congregations in and near Norwich and established them on Congregational lines. Be afterwards, with 60 others fled to Holland and there formed a church. There also he published a book, proclaiming his ecclesias- tical principles. Having suffered many years on account of them, he at length recanted them and returned to his former allegiance, to the Anglican church. Although, however, the leader abandoned his prineioles, his follow- ers clung to them and promoted them. Thus the Tollovrere of Brown were men of truer, and nobler type

_ than Brown himself, among whom were the two clergy- men referred to by Mr. VVillis in his lecture, Elias Thacker and John Copping. They were convicted in June 1583 for dispersing the books written by Robert Brown and Robert Harrison. Copping was further accused with having said that the Queen was perjured. Here then we have the whole amount of the plot against the Queen, for which according to the Rector, the State and not the church put these men to death. Their only offence was a protest against the Queen's supremacy as gover- nor of the church in these realms. In all other respects these clergymen were as loyal to the crown and rule of Elizabeth as Mr. Willis himself is to the crown and rule of Victoria ; and also they were men "Sound in the doctrinal articles of the Church of England, and of unblemished lives." We are justified therefore, in declaring that it is a defamation of the memory of loyal Christian men, who actually as Neal says "died by their principles "to speak of them as hung, not for their opinions, but on account of sedition against the Queen. Our chief business, however is with the three other men, who have been similarly branded as traitors by the Rec- tor, but who are recorded as martyrs for principle upon the page of history,namely Henry Barrowe, John Green- wood, and John Penry. After givieg a short biography ef the three martyrs, and their trials, and imprisonment Mr. Yonge said for six years in prison Barrowe and Greenwood employed themselves in writing on scraps of paper as they had the chance, tracts setting forth their views. But were not these tracts dangerous? Di i thew not preach sedßition and treason? Nothing of the kind- ! 'inn writers, arrv- and Greenwood, expressly ,-.>dis- avowed all part in any move:J....a IL.. actuodelllug State Church. " Our purpose" said they "is not to meddle with the reformation of the State, otherwise than by our prayers to God and refraining from all things that are not according to God's law." They simply declared in their tracts their distinctive doctrine concerning the church of Christ. At length, Barrowe and Greenwood, along with three others, were put on their trial at the Old Bailey, in London, " for writing and publishing sundry seditious books and pamphlets, tending to the slander of the Queen and Government. This, as was well known at the time,was simply a ruse of Archbishop Whitgift's, to pass the odium of their intended death from himself to the civil power. As a writer of the period says, "though Mr. Barrowe and Mr. Greenwood were condemned for dis• turbance of the State,yet this would have been pardoned and their lives spared, if they would have promised to come to church." Sentence of death was passed on March 23, whereupon sundry ecclesiastics were sent to persuade them to recant. They were not, however, the men to buy liberty, or even life at the price of soul and conscience. The authorities next tried the influence of actual confronting with death, and so sent them to Tyburn,the place of executien where they were tied each by his neck to the gibbet, as though directly to be exe- cuted. In that posture they were told; to speak to the people ; which they did, protesting their byalty to the State, and praying heaven to bless both Queen and country. Just as they finished and were expecting in- stant death,a reprieve arrived from the Queen, and they were carried back to prison. On April 6, they were suddenly once again sent to Tyburn and hanged. At the place of execution they gave such testimonies of unfeigned piety and loyalty to the Qtieen, that even the chaplain who had attended their last moments reported their words to the Queen. She repented that she had permitted their death. And well she might. For no one knew better than the Queen how these men had been sacrificed for no crime, but only because they had dared to hold conscientious religious opinions in defiance of all human interference. John Penry, a Welshman, was also put to death about a month later. Pa 1592, he finally abandoned his connection with the Church of England and joined the Congrentionalists in South walk. All his movements were watched, his enemies were bent upon his death, and cnly waited the opportunity that should give him into their hands. That moment came on March 22nd, 1593. His arrest was

made at Stepney by the help of the Vi- car of the pariah. Now this young Christian's indict- ment before his judges was laid not upon any book he had published or any word he had spoken, but upon matters gleaned from some private notes found by the Archbishop's officers among his papers. They were notes of a draft petition to the Queen, upon the state of re- ligion, and claiming toleration in matters of faith and worship. These notes were construed into treason, and on May 21 he was convicted, on May 25 he was sen- tenced, and four days later he suffered the martyr's death. The whole thing was grossly illegal then, and could never be possible now. The sorrowful thing is that good men can be found, such as the Rector who can bolster up this unrighteous and intolerant deed by calling it a sentenc,e for treason. It was nothing of the kind. Penry died a martyr for his faith, which is our faith. Surely what these men could die for, we can live for! To forsake the scriptural Congregational iaith,and the Nonconformist freedom, in order to take up with a sacerdotal and Romanising and State, enthralled church would be to forsake liberty for bondage—to put our religious clock back 300 years.
