MAG-AZIN.EB, &c.

--- The first monthly part of a new illustrated edition of " Picturesque America" was issued on the 27th March, by Messrs. Cassell and Co., who naturally consider the occasion most opportune for the introduction of a pictorial delineation of American life, and scenery, in consequence of the keen interest likely to be aroused by The World's Fair in everything American. The work when complete will contain 48 steel engravings, and several hundred engravings on wood, from original drawings prepared specially by artists, who were com- missioned to visit the most picturesque districts of the United States. The " Magazine of Art " for April is an excellent number, the principal article in it being a description by Mr. Ryles, of Temple Newsam, Mrs. Meynell Ing.ram's famous house near Leeds. The frontispiece is an Original Etching, called " Shere," by Percy Robertson, R.P.E., a beautifully soft, and realistic picture. Along with these are several attractive papers on other sub- jects, all well-written and profusely illustrated. Another capital shillingsworth is " the Portrait Gal- lery," containing cabinet-size portraits of Dr. Hubert Parry, Miss Marim Terry, and Mr. Bernard Shaw, each photograph being accompanied hy a biographical sketch. We have also received " The Quiver," " Cassell's Magazine," and " Little Folks," each one specially at- tractive, and the very best of its kind. For the scienti- fically inclined there are issued in menthly parts— " Figuier's Popular Scientific Works." "Our earth and its story," " Science for all," and " Storehouse of general information," all by Cassell and Co., who cer- tainly endeavour to please the public taste in every direction. "The Century Magazine" for April opens with an article on "The Chicago Anarchists of 1886," written by the judge who presided at the trial ; the principal events connected with it are illustrated, and portraits given of the principal men concerned in it. There is also a most interesting biographical sketch of Margaret Fuller, and another one of Queen Anne. Charming bits of verse, complete stories, serials, .te., all combine to make the magazine popular and instructive. The Newspaper Press Directory 'for 1893, issued by Messrs. C. Mitchell and Co., the well-known advertising contractors, London, is a wonderful compilation to be sold for two shillings. It net only contains a full list of English, Scotch and Irish newspapers, and periodicals but also of Colonial, Indian, and Continental publica- tions. It contains, in addition, a good deal of literary matter, including articles on the' The Rights and Liabilities of Newspapers ' and Digest of Statutes relating to the Newspaper Press,' and ' A Short History of the Australian Press.' There is also an admirable newspaper map of the United Kingdom.

WESLEYAN FOREIGN MISSIONARY ANN IVE RSA.RY.

The Wesleyan Foreign Missionary Anniversary, was held in Bold-street Chapel, Warrington, cn Sunday and Monday. On Sunday the Rev. T. G Seib, of Garston, (late of China), preached in the morning and evening, and on the following day a meeting was held in the chapel. Mr. W. Gleave occupied the chair at the meeting, and he was supported by the three circuit ministers, the Rev. T. G. Selby, Rev. T. Moscrop from Ceylon, Dr. Starkey Smith, and Messrs. W. Silcock, E. Richard- son and W. Bennett. Tile proceedings were opened by a hymn, followed by prayer by Rev. T.Ross. The Rev. A. Freeman then read the annual report. He was glad to Bay that last year there was an increase in Warrington alone of £4 8s 51 Miss Henrietta Brown had collected £6 45., Miss Freeman £l5 Os 6d, and Miss Weaver £8 6s 6d. From public collections they had re- ceived from Bold-street £3l 19s scl; Bewsey-road £l5 Os 7d ; Moore £l5 25..; Whitley £6 15s 9d ; Antrobus £6 14s 91; Appleton £B9s 3d ; Padgste £26 14s 3d ; Hatton £7 18s 2d ; Latchford £5 7s 3d ; Martinscroft £2 Es 2d ; Sankey Bridges £7 15s 7d ; Mill Lane £2 12 91; Stockton Heath £2 Be sd. These, with money from other sources made a total for the circuit of £246 6s 9d, being an increase of £ll 8s 7d on the previous year. The money collected by children was also larger during the past year than, with one exception, during the past•ten years. Mr. Freeman closed by a reference to the late Mr. Wm. Barlow. They met that night, he said, under the shadow of a great loss. A voice long familiar at the foreign missionary anniversaries was missing. Mr. Wm. Barlow had been for many years in the office of circait treasurer for foreign missions. In the capacity of secretary he (Mr. Freeman) was brought into frequent contact with Mr. Ba- rlow, and could bear witness to the courtesv,promptitude and thoroughness with which he discharged the duties of his office. Mr. Barlow had held other positions of useful- ness in the church. He had been a willing contributor to the funds of the society, and in many ways showed his sympathy with its operations. His sudden death was admonitory to all.—The Chairman expressed his hearty sympathy with the objects of the meeting, and wished the foreign missions every success.—Rev. T. G. Selby next addressed the meeting. He said he represented one of the most difficult of all the mission fields in which they were called to labour. Part of the difficulty was political, and the people of this coun- try could do something towards lessening it. He had no faith whatever in the man who professed his strong- est sympathy with the proclaiming of the gospel to the heathen, and yet at the same time gave his vote to the pledged itself to keep up a temple of ini- quity y which ic thhat of the opium trade. They ought never to let their prayers and votes go against each other. The English were not the first to introduce the trade into China, but they were the first to force it npon the Chin- ese. This caused great difficulty to the missionaries who went there, as the Chinese knew perfectly well that when the missionary came, by and by the merchant would follow in his footsteps, and that meant increasing facilities for the opium trade. Another prejudice against white people was caused by the French miBsionarieslwho were hacked by the French Government, and interfered frequently with the rights and privileges of the Man- darins. The Chinese also, could not forget that the English had fought them and beaten them, and It was always a difficult task for a missionary to go among a con- quered people. But in spite of all these draw-backs, the number of Christians in China was increasing, and he Noked for great results in a very few years. The Rev. T. MOSCROP, who has only arrived from Ceylon a few weeks ago, gave an interesting account of the Buddhists of Ceylon, and spoke encouragingly of the mission work in Ceylon. He said that the Buddhist faith had been tried, and been found wanting. The Christian religion supplied what was wanted,and it was spreading over the island. On the motion of the Rev. A. FREEMAN, second- ed by Dr. Starkey Smith, the following gentlemen were elected to the local missionary Committee t—secretaries, Rev. A. Freeman, and Mr.R. Smith ; general committee, Dr. Starkey nw;th. J.P., Messrs. g Seaman, J. Bennett, %V. Uleave, W. Hickman, J. Shaw Green, J. W. Hay- ward, J.P.,C. H. Kerry, W. Y. Lawton, W. G. Nicholson E. Richardson, J. Summersgill. J. Taylor, and E. Wea- ver. The treasurer in the place of the late Mr. Wm. Barlow has not yet been appointed. A vote of thanks to the collectors, deputation, choir, and chairman closed the meeting.

WIN WICK DISTRICT TECHNICAL

INSTRUCTION COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the Winwick District Technical Instruction Committee was held on Wednesday, in the B ewsey Chambers. Mr. J. C. Sutton, C.C., pre- sided, and read letters apologising for their absence from the Rev. G. F. Roberes, Rev. J. J. Langham, and the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, and Mr. Chas. Parker, Penketh. After a few opening remarks by the Chairman, Mr. S. Sutton (sec.) read his report, which stated :—"The classes upon the whole have been fairly well attended. The ambulance, chemistry, and cookery classes had been particularly so. In some ot the centres there has been a failing off in the dress- making classes, but probably this is due to the fact that the population of rural districts fluctuates very little, land that pupils in good attendance the previous session have with but a few exceptions, failed to rejoin." The secretary further read an elaborate tabulated statement of the classes in each division,giving the attendance and other details. He also read a return of the expenditure and liabilit'es incurred in each centre during the session and a general financial statement showing the total thereof to be a little more than £23B.—Mr. Dignum said that the classes might be viewed all round as satisfactory, and that the benefits resulting would show the money to have been well spent. In alluding to the falling off in the dressmaking classes, he considered that the secre- tary's explanation was correct.—The Rev. Canon Pen- rhyn concurred.—The Secretary explained that whereas Burtonwood in the previous session could not be stirred, it had now become the strongest and almost best. attended centre.—Votes of thanks were passed to the 1 treasurer, Mr.Dignum,and other officers, and after some further routine business, the meeting ended.

Smith, Major Waring was unanimously elected people's warden.—The following gentlemen were elected sidesmen :—Messrs. J. Ashall,A. Bennett, R. Dickenson, W. Booth, T. Forster, E. Hard- man, W. Smith, J. Latham, J. Edwardson, J. Goff, D. Hurst, J. Holland, J. Hutchinson, S. Kidd, F. H. Pank- hurst, J. Patten, R. Riley, J. Shuttleworth, C. A. Sed- don, Knowles, R. Campbell, and A. Leys.—Messrs. A. Bennett and W. Christie were appointed lay representa- tives. In answer to inquiries it was stated that the estimate for the vicarage was £1,409, but extras had , brought the total cost to £l,BOO. Votes of thanks were accorded to the various officers of the Church, and a hearty welcome was extended to the Rev. EL E. Samp- son, the curate.

THE JERUSALEM B A.Z A. AR AT

WAR RINGTON GYAINASI IT3I.

" Are you going to the Jerusalem Bazaar 2" was a question which has. been very much asked during the week, and it has, it may be said, been very much answered ha the affirmative. The bazaar which has been organized and arranged by the Roman Catholics of the town was opened on Tuesday afternoon. It represents a Street of Jerusalem and has been fitted up with a consider- able amount of artistic skill, which does credit alike to those who have had to do with the arranging of the stalls, and those who have had to do with the difficult task of building the temporary shops. The effect produced was very striking, especially during the evenings when under the influence of the electric apparatus supplied by Mr. Morton. The stalls have all been stocked with a great variety of articles supplied by the ladies. The ladies indeed, have undoubtedly played the chief part in the whole affair, for the teeming display of saleable goods on the stalls was alone an eloquent testimony of their generous labours. During a speech which he made, Father Cody in alluding to the work of the ladies, facetiously said " they have hitherto been doing the sewing, and now let us hope they will reap the harvest." And had they not reaped a harvest—and a very satisfactory one—it would not have been because they did not throw sufficient zest into their work ; it would have been because the gentlemen were disinclined to become the source from which the harvest , was to be reaped. But the gentlemen, and also the ladies who commanded exchequers, were generous ; and under the soothing refrains sent down from the gallery by Mr. Down's band, they "considered not the lucre" when pressed to purchase anything from a gipsey table at a stall,to a ticket for a raffle. Indeed,as Father Craven put it, in some of his remarks" what chloroform is to a man who is having a limb amputated, that is music to those who buy at a bazaar."

TETE OBJECT OF TRH BAZAAR,

A word about the object of the bazaar. Within a very short space ef time the representatives of Roman Catho- licism in Warrington have made themselves manifest by the achievement of several striking additions and beauti- fications to their local churches. In addition to costly alterations which have been made to St. Mary's Church, Buttertnarket-street, and St. Mary's, Latchford, a new mission has been built at Bank Quay,and a site on which is to be erected a new church,in the same neighbourhood, has been purchased, and last, but certainly riot the least important, was the acquisition of the magnificent altar , recently opened at St. Alban's, Bewsey-street. Except with regard to the latter source of expenditure,no public appeal has been made for the monetary assistance. The 1 debt entailed by the erection of the new altar necessitated I this however, and so, f r the secood time in the history of the Roman Catholic Church in Warrington, it was decided to obtain the necessarymoney—E6so—by means of a bazaar. And from the thronged aspect of the" Street of Jerusalem," and the presence of generous purchasers at the commencement of the bazaar, it might reasonably be conjectured that the four days' sale would fulfil the object for which it was promoted. Father Feeney in con- templating the erection of the altar, was conscious of the great expense which was about to be entailed; but he was assured by b is congregation that although the sum was too much to be met by ordinary offertories, be might rest assured that if he would undertake the organization of a bazaar, all necessary support would be forthcoming.

THE INFIRMARY AND DISPENSARY BALL,

The annual ball in aid of the funds of the War- rington Infirmary and Dispensary was held in the Assembly Room, at the Lion Hotel, on Tuesday evening. The attendance this year was not so large as on previous occasions, due, no doubt, to the lateness in the season. The floor of the ballroom was all that could be desired. Extensive decorations have been car- ried out by the new proprietors of the hotel (Messrs. P. Walker and Co., Limited), which were further enhanced by the addition of flowers and plants. Dancing com- menced soon after nine o'clock and was kept u until early morning. The musical ail p arrangements were under the charge of Mr. R. H. Pearson. A splendid band of eight instruments, including a harp, was in attend- ance, and gave eveiy satisfaction. The ante-room and other rooms hut been decorated by Messrs. S. E. and It. Johnson. The catering was in the hands of Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Taylor. The stewards were the Mayor of Warrington (Alderman E. Greenall). Captain E. Sker- rnw, and Mr. John White. Valuable assistance was • given by Mr. E. Gaskell Davies, who ably discharged the duties of hon. secretary. The following is a list of those present :—Mr. and Miss Andrews, Mrs. and Miss Blain, Mr. A. S. Bleckly, Mr. C. 11. and Miss Broadbent, Mrs. and Miss Brabazon, Mr. John Coxon, Mr. Cookson, Mr. It. MR. JABEZ BALE° U R'S EXTRADITION

DECISION OF THE ARGENTINE GOVERNMENT. BUENOS AYRES, WEDNESDAY.—T is" Times of Argen- tina "states that the British Ministm.'s request for the extradition of Mr. Jabez Spencer Balfour has been re- fused by the Government there, owing to the absence of an Extradition Treaty. The claim would have been granted were reciprocity in a similar case promised by the British Government, but the British Minister was uable to accept that condition, aad the negociations therefore dropped. A news agency states that it has received confirmation of the information that two ladies left England some weeks since to join Jabez Spencer Bilfour in the Argen- tine, but neither of these was Mrs. Balfour. This lady' as announced during the proceedings in the bankruptcy court in connection with the receiving order granted 'against the ex-member for Burnley, is confined in a lunatic asylum.

EXAMINER,

BATURbAV, APRIL 15, 1893

A Notable ONE of the most notable contribu- 6Pecch tions to the debate on Home Rule was the speech delivered in the 11°118° °I Commons on Tuesday evening by Mr. rtiklellAsL DAvrrr. Mr. DAVITT is a typical Irish- a,nci he is perhaps, to a greater extent than a " °tiler Nationalist member of Parliament, a representative of those Irishmen, who, we are lie°llstaritlY told by Unionist orators, are bitterly -r,°stile in their hearts to England and everything 12.tngli'h.tion It ia impressed upon us with tedious seb vim le their" iteration 15 these men, however they may dis- real feelings at the present time, will, ll°4lo Rule is once conceded, make use of .f their -el? Powers for the purpose of injuring, and, 13°ssible, of humiliating this country. Mr. sUay.itt has again and again been hold up to repro- bation as one of the most prominent and the most influential of these so-called " irreconcilable" Irishmen. It is, therefore, interesting to hear 'What Mr. DAVITT has to say for himself as to the spirit and temper in which he approaches and will accept the Home Rule measure of the Government. Of his honesty and candour there can be no ques- tion. THE demonstration which was pro- liasco. 'noted by the brewers and publicans of London on Saturday against the 143°a1 1r the • eto Bill was not a success. In fact, from f4il P°lnt of view of the promoters it was a dismal tlieur". Instead of demonstrating the reality of WrAth of the workingmen of London against

===== 0002601_18930415_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
the Bill, it very clearly showed that tho feeling which brings mass meetings together is confined pretty much to those who are in favour of the Bill. A great deal of trouble and expense had been incurred in organizing the demonstration ; many brass bands had been engaged, and no doubt those persuasive influences which brewers and publicans are known to have at command,were freely used— all with the disheartening result that when the meagre processions reached Trafalgar Square the publican and his proteges found that they were completely outnumbered by the "teetotallers." A considerable amount of censure has been meted out to the temperance party—in some cases by their friends—for the action they took in connec- tion with this demonstration. Some of the inci- dents of the affair are to be regretted, such as the tearing of some of the banners of the processionists. Banners which bore such foolish and inconsequential inscriptions as, "Why deprive working bees of their Sunday beer F" might very well have been left to float in the breeze,ancl to bring ridicule on those who bore them. But then these violent collisions were, for the most part, provoked by demonstrators who , wished to oust others from the places they had secured. Over and above this, it should be kept in mind that the disorder has been much magnified by those who are chagrined at the result, and by others who would like nothing better than to see the populace of London deprived of the privelege of meeting in Trafalgar Square. That the affair was far from reaching the dimensions of a riot is proved by the statement of the Home *rotary that no personal injury of any kind had been sus- tained, and that perambulators with their occu- pants and attendants circulated freely in the neighbourhood. Had the supporters of the pro- posed temperance legislation of the Government not attended the gathering and out-voted the promoters, the meeting would have been held up all over the country as voicing the sentiments of the people of London. As it is it helps to show how little of real force there is behind the loud bluster of the liquor trade.

FOR those who are addicted to beer, Te h Hull tobacco, and beef, the position of a Strike. "free" labourer at Hull qlocks during the present strike mast be an enviable one. "Free labourer," by the way, is the name given to this class of men by the Federation of em- plovers ; they are called " blacklegs " by other workmen. According to the special correspondent ,of the "Daily News" the "free labourers" are I "living in clover " so far as material comforts go, though their freedom is of such a nature that they are confined to temporary berths in the docks,and dare not show their faces in the streets of Hull. They can earn three pounds a week by working overtime, and, in the way of victuals and stimu- lants, they are allowed a pound-and-a-half of I beef a day, two pints of b2er, and two ounces of tobacco. As to the manner of men these are that the shipowners are engaging to replace their unionist workmen, the following colloquy between the correspondent and one of the "free " labourers will give some idea. " Tobacco, beer, beef, and so cm, all right F" inquired the correspondent of fa. gay rollicking fellow. " Oh, ay," said he. " The beef is prime and.we get more of it than we want. we get our two ounces of ba.cey every day, and , that's good too—leastway I have no complaint to make—rand so is the beer. There is only one , thing wrong with it—there's too little of it." " You get two pints a day, don't you F" " Yes but I wish they would make it two gallons. I could drink every blooming drop of it." And with the word "gallons "on his lip the gay young man lounged away to the ships.

IT is said that an extremely interesting " find " has been made in the convent on Mount Sinai. It consists of nothing less than a Syrian text of all the four Gospels which " among all preserved testimonies contains the oldest authenticated texts of the Gospels." Only fragments of the Syrian text have, it appears, hitherto been known, these being in a single manuscript in the British Museum, and in two leaves of it which went to Berlin. The honour of making the discovery be- longs to an Englishwoman, Mrs. LEWIS, who was accompanied by her sister, Mrs. GrasoN, both of these ladies being conversant with Oriental languages. The manuscript, it seems, was in an exceedingly dilapidated condition, but the leaves were separated with the steam of a tea kettle,and the whole of the text of from throb to four hun- dred pages were photographed. In the work of deciphering the ladies were assisted by 'Professor HARRIS, who had hastened to Mount Sinai on , hearing of the discovery, and the party sojourned in the ctesert convent for a period of forty days. It is interesting to note that Professor HARMS reports that the last twelve verses of St. MARX nra missing from the newly-found text, and that no substitute is offered for them.

FATALITY AT WESTON QUARRIES..

The Coroner, Mr. J. E. Worsley, held an inquest at Runcorn Police Court, on Thursday, on the body of John Basnett, aged 68 years, a quarryman, of 10, Hartley-st., Runcorn. The evidence showed that on Tuesday after- noon the deceased, who was employed at the Ship Canal Quarries, at Weston was engage with several other men at the bottom of the quarry, when a quantity of " rubble " and stones of considerable size fell down from the top of the quarry upon Basnett,killing him instantly. Another man named Fred Friss had a narrow escape.— Mr. Wm. Bushell, the manager of the quarries, said no blastirv, had taken place at the spot whence the stones fell for some weeks, for that and all the loose stones were at that time removed. The state of the glass trade is still causing serious anxiety to St. Helens manufacturers, who are putting in new machinery at enormous cost. Messrs. DISEASE AND DEATH IN WIDNES.

Dr. O'Keeffe, the medical officer of health, pre- sented his annual report to Widnes Town Council on Tuesday. It stated that the number of deaths registered in the borough of Widnes daring the year 1892 was 598, and calculating on a population of 30,000 persons to the middle of 1892, this gives a death-rate of 19.93 per thousand for the year. The deaths from the eight principal zymotie diseases num- bered,s3 or 1.76 per thousand of population, and were as fellows :—Smallnox, 0 ; measles, 4 ; scarlet fever, 3; typhoid fever, 25; typhus fever, 1 ; diarrhcea and dysentery, 17 ; erysipelas, 2 ; diphtheria, 1 ; total 53. The deaths of children under one year, including prema- ture births, numbered 199 or 6.63 per thousand, and of those between the ages of one and five years 93 or 3.10 per thousand. The number of cases treated in the ' ;Infectious Diseases Hospital during the year was 46. Of these 11 ended fatally. The numb,:r of cases treated in the Accident Hospital was 66 ; of these 54 recovered and 12 died. He had visited both hospitals frequently and had invariably found the management satisfactory. The births registered in Widnes during 1892 numbered 1185, or 39.50 per thousand of population. Twenty- nine inquests were held during this year. No case of smallpox occurred during the year. The scavenging con- tinues to be carried out in an efficient manner. As to the water supply, the quality of the water according to analysis by Dr. Campbell-Brown is excellent, and the available supply is abundant. Efficient measures ha,te been taken by the United Alkali Company for suppressing the escape of sulphuretted hydrogen, and the nuisance from that source has now been reduced to a minimum.

TIIIIN.GS HEARD AND SEEN IT RUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

The election of Runcorn Guardians resulted in no surprises. The retiring members deservedly held the foremost places on the list. Past services seem to have been recognised in this Instance. The new members are Messrs. Thomas Handley, Samuel Yarwood and David H. Jones. The two former go to Dutton for the first time, but Mr. Tones has served his apprenticeship during a very lively period. There is plenty of scope for new ,„:,lood on the Board.

Runcorn Commi:!sion;dra and Widnes Corporation are of op.e with regard to the proposed vehicula: °ridge Wass the Mersey. Both authori- OA &cline to advance beyond their offer of SBO,OOO towards the cost and considering thesize of the two towns ,and the rates with which they are burdened the offers were very generous. The County of Lancaster and the County of Chester would benefit ovally with the districts in the vicinity of the bridge and it is most decidedly a work for the counties to undertake.

There was considerable feeling imported into the disciu-sion at Widnes Town Council on Tuesday as to the disposal of the gas profits. The " heat " was altogether unnecessary, and it is a pity that the Councillors wko apparently held briefs, for the pro- perty owners should have taken umbrage at an in- nocent reference to absentee landlords. There are good arguments on both sides of the question. It certainly seems reasonable that the puulic lighting should not be charged to the rates uuderall the cir- cumstances of the case and the Council rejected Alderman Timmis's motion by 14 votes to seven. The representatives of all the large works voted with Mr. Timmis, which to use a common phrase, shows on which side their bread is buttered.

ELECTIONS IN RUNCORN UNION.

The first triennial election of Guardians of the Runcorn Union was concluded on Tuesday, when the counting of votes took place at Dutton Work- house. There were contests in only three town- ships—Appleton, Frods ham Lordship and .Runcorn. In the former place the two retiring guardians were opposed by Mr. Charles W. Davenport, who succeeded in headipg the poll with 317 votes. Mr. Joseph Knowles obtainiA 221 votes and Mr. John Whitloa 108 votes. the two first named are therefore elected. At Frodshain Lordship the opposition to Mr. Thomag Lewis, who has represented the township for many years, was ridiculed from the commencement and the result shows not with- out reason. While Mr. Lewis secured 191 votes, his opponent Mr. T. If. Hargre eves totalled only 17, a most crushing defeat. In Runcorn there were twelve candid- ates for seven seats but the interest in the contest was not very great, as may be judged from the fact that 1,050 papers were returned blank. The polling resulted as .!,c,';`Toq4 ; thP. iiNgrniheitaii7P,aol Allred R. Norman, 827; Thomas Lea, 893; Samuel Yarwood, 495; D. H. Jones, 408- G. R. Kirkham, 393 ; B. Kniv.eton, 370; S, Coventry, 306; S. Temperley, 225; (i. Tome, 121. The electi.n was conducted by Mr. John Ashton, clerk to the Union as returning officer. The counting occupied the whole of Taesday and it was not until nearly ten o'clock at night that information was received in Runcorn of the result.

M. TIVADAR NACHEZ AT RUNCORN.

It is very seldom that a small provincial town has the opportunity of seeing and hearing the great masters of music and art, and Runcorn people were repecially favoured on Monday evenang in having the privilege of listening to one who by common consent is described as the most brilliant violinist of the present time. M. Tivadar Nachez' has per- formed before all the crowned heads in Europe, and before the critical audiences of the European capi- tals, and on each occasion with unqualified success. Al- though Runcorn Public Hall was not crowded on Monday eveumg, there was yet a large audience, and it may safely be said that never did M. Nachez perform before a; more enthusiastic company. Those who came to criticise' stayed to wonder at the marvellous command the execu- tant had over his instrument, and when he had com- pleted his fourth solo a scene of excitement never before witnessed at a concert in that hall took place. When after applause had continued for nearly five minutes, M. Nachrz, who had twice previously responded to en- cores, came forward to again obey their desire for an encore, the audience gave one simultaneous shout that was almost electrical. Mr. S. Liddle was an admirable accompanist. Included in the programme were songs by Mrs. Mytton Thornycroft, the Rev. J. Elston, Der. Millward, and the Rev. A. T. Powley, and a pianoforte solo be the Hon. Blanche Tollemache all of whom acquitted themselves most efficiently, and to the evident enjoyment of the auditory. The concert had been ar- ranged by the Rev. H. B. Blogg, in aid of Five Crosses Mission Church.

-.4•_. WIDNES FIRE B RIGAD E.

The Superintendent (Mr. I. Carr) presented hia annual report to the Town Council on Tuesday The total number of fires that have occurred during the year within the borough, exclusive of chimney fires, is seven, outside the borough one. Three of the fires were serious, and the remainder slight. The brigade has had six calls when on each occasion they extinguished the fires. The estimated total loss from fires was £7OO. The estimated value of the property at risk was £111,600. With the present increasing consump- tion of water for works purposes, which is very irregular, and at a time when a number of works are drawing heavily on the mains together, the pressure falls so low that it would at such a time be of little use if required for fire purposes. The pressure can of course be augmented by the existing manual engine, but not nearly so effectively as it could be by a steam fire engine. Further, the former method is very expensive and as a ,rule very unsatisfactory on account of the difficulty of dealing with the men that are hired to work the pumps. The Superintendent would therefore ask the Committee to take into consideration the advisability of providing the brigade with a steam fire engine. One fireman has resigned during the year, leaving the total strength of the brigade fourteen, or one short of the full comple- ment. Nine of the firemen are residing in the fire station cottages. The condition of the brigade as regards efficiency and discipline is good. Arrangements are being made for the firemen to have an elementary course of lectures under the auspices of the St. John Ambulance Association. I suggested in my last annual report that the arrangements for calling the brigade would be more complete if a system of electric call bells was introduced, connecting the Town flail, Police Station, Mr. Lee's stables with the firemen's cottages, but nothing has so far been done in the matter. The police as in previous year haw: rendered the brigade able assistance whers. attending fires,

THE RURIAL RETURN.

The TOWn Clerk submitted the return of the number : of interments which had taken place at each of the, various burial places in Widnes during the past three years. It appeared that at Farnworth there had been 764 interments and at St. Mary's Parish Church 5f5, while at St. Bedes there were no statistics. At Farn- worth the average number of bodies interred in public ' and private graves was three or four adults4-Linore ' children according to age. At St. Mary's Parish church' the average was live. No return was made from St: Bedes. At Farnworth the area at present available unoccupied burial space was 3,600 square yards, exclii- sive of paths and of incidental small space i 1 older pa'r,!.' of yard, and it was estimated that 4,500 bodies coirld still be buried there—considerably more of children.' 'At St. Mary's there were 716 square yards available, and it St. Bedes 450 square yards, and seven or eight ncros could be added At necessary.—Councillor HodgkihSon said he would be prepared to deal with the question at the next meeting.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

AN UNHAPPY MARRIAGB. At the Warrington Borough Court on Saturday, William Dumbell, jun., was again brought up on the charge of unlawfully leaving his wife and children chargeable to the Warrington Union. Mr. Shearer said the case had been adjourned for 14 days to see if the parties could come to satisfactory terms, but they were now in the same position as they were before. The defendant had brought the 16 shillings owing to the Union, and was prepared to pay. He (Ur. Shearer) therefore withdrew the case. Mrs. Dumbell, said her husband had been on the Monday previous, to make an arrangement with her but she declined to make any arrangement. His abuse had been very great, he had threatened to take her life. The magistrates dismissed the case on the understanding that the defendant paid the money owing. "4 BANKED " IN TRH POOR Box. Four youths n med Istah Deakin (20), labourer, 23, Beswick-st., Thomas Ellison (17), 17. Percy-st. Henry Ellison (15), 20, Percy-st., James Jackson (14), 26, Percy-et. were charged at the Borough Court, on Mon- day, with having played at cards for money on Sunday, the 2nd April, ia Wellfield-'t. From the evidence it appeared that the four defendants were playing" Ban- ker" behind a tip when they were espied by P.C. Bar- low, who immediately pulled his tunic and helmet off and caught them redharaded and took the cards and 2s. 2d. in money. When asked by the magistrates who the money belonged to, the prisoners declined to own it, whereupon it was ordered to be put in the poor box. Deakin was fined 53. and costs, T. Ellison, ss. including eats, and the other two defendants 2.5. 6d. each. BROTHERS IN ADVERSITY. At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, James Hopkins and George Hopkins were charged on a warrant, with unlawfully and wilfully committing damage to a plate glass window of the Three Pigeons, to the extent of 455., on April 10. James was also charged with being disorderly and refus ng to quit the Three Pigeons tan the same date. William Chalmers, landlord of Three Pigeons said that en Weanesday prisoners came to that public-house. George stopped at the door way, and James came inside. As he was drunk, witness refused to serve him, and told him to go out. He refused, and witness had to put him out. The two prisoners then tried to force open the door. Witness held the door to keep them out, when the prisoner George lifted up his arm and pushed it through the window. The two pris- oners then ran away. Fred. Binks, barman, corrobor- ated. Detective Monaghan said that when he arrested prisoners, George stated that he broke the window,and not James. The magistrates fined prisoner James 10s. and costs or 14 days for refusing to quit, and George 455. and costs or one month's hard labour, for breaking the window. SHOCKING ASSAULT ON AN OLD WOMAN. At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, Thomas Kelly, was charged with maliciously inflicting bodily injuries on Honor Fleming, on Sunday the 9th inst. The Chief Constable said that complainant Honor Fleming was a widow, aged 64 years, living with prisoner as his housekeeper, in James-st., off Buttermarket-st. Pri- soner was a widower, aged 34 years. and had married complainant's daughter, who died two years ago, lea!- jog two children. About 10.45 on Sunday night, a noise like the breaking of a window and the crash of glass was heard coming from prisoner's house by some per- sons living opposite. They also beard cries of " Murder " and" Police." They went to the house and burst open the door, and found prisoner holding the woman by the throat, almost strangling her underneath the stairs. He was also kicking her. They pulled prisoner away, and found complainant bleeding profusely from the mouth. Prisoner was in a violent temper and was not sober. Complainant was taken away into the house of Mrs. Smith who lived opposite, and put to bed. On Monday, the matter was reported to Inspector Spinks by com- plainant's son, and a warrant was taken out against the prisoner, and he was arrested on Monday night. Dr. Peacocke said that he was called in on Monday to see complainant. Her left eye was considerably bruised and swol'en. There were bruises above the shoulder and in the ribs on the left side, and there were scratches all raver her face and neck, and black marks on the shoul- der. Prisoner was remanded until Friday. Kelly was again brought before the magistrates on Fri. day—Honor Fleming,who was very weak, and had to be supplied with a chair gave full details of the assault complained of, stating that she had been knocked down On the hearth stone, being pushed with her head against the door, and finally he had peshed her into tiff pantry, where he was beating her when she was rescued. Tim charge was altered at this stage into one of aggravated assault, to which prisoner, on the advice of Mr. Browns who appeared for him, pleaded guilty. Mr. Browne said that his client had had a good character in the army and at his work, and had been teetotal until last Sun- day, when he broke down. He had a quarrel with the old woman about a bill when he got home, and this was the cause of the row. The magistrate, considered this to be a very had case, and fined pr:eoner £5 including costs or one month's hard labour.

jumped on the line when there was a train backing into the station, and witness had to help him on to the plat- form, and told him he must not cross the line again. Prisoner became very quarrelsome and struck and kicked witness. Prisoner was tined ss.and costs for being drunk, and 10s. including costs, for the assault, or seven days' , hard labour for each charge. WEDNESDAY.—Before Mr. John Fairclough (chairman), and Mr. John Geddes. DRUNK AND DISORDERLY.—George Walters, Butter- market-street, on Tuesday night, ss. including costa or

7 days. THIIRSDAY.—Bef ore Alderman Sutton (chairman) and Councillor J. Fairclough. DESERTION OF FAXlLY.—William Waiting was charged with deserting his wife and three children and leaving them chargeable on the Warrington Union on April 6. Mr. Shearer said that prisoner's wife applied for relief on March 7,and when he visited the house he found them to be destitute. Prisoner had left them and gone to Derby, from where he sent several small sums, but in- sufficient to keep them. They were admitted into the Workhouse on April 7, and had incurred an expense of £2 4s. A warrant was issued against prisoner, and he was brought to Warrington from Derby. The case was adjourned for a month, on prisoner promising to find work and pay the expense incurred by the Union. AN OLD CASE OF DRUNBENNESS.—Frank O'Brien was I charged on a warrant, with being drunk and disorderly - in Buttermarket-street on May 24, 1892. Prisoner was ' summoned for the offence at the instance of P.C. Wheat- ley, but failed to appear, and that officer arrested him on I Wednesday night. Prisoner was fined 108. including costs or 7 days' imprisonment. Senoot CASES.—Charles Cherley, Bakehouse-yard, Latchford, fined 55.; Martin Carey, Old-road, Latchford, ss. ; Patrick Nestor, Dial-street, 55. ; John Rooney or Forster, Cockhedge-lane, 55.; John MacDonald, Rose and Crown-street, 10th appearance, 53. ; Wm. Farrington, Bewsey-road, sa. ; the latter defendant has already paid over £5 in fines. Mr. George Riley, school attendance inspector pros ecuted. FRlDAL—Before Alderman Wright (chairman), Dr. Gornall and Councillor Roberts. DRUNEENNESS.—Lawrencee Mack, in Bridge-st., OD Thursday, 55. including costs ; Joseph Ward, of School Brow, on April 9th, 203. and costs or one month's hard labour. SLEEPING OUT.—For this offence in Golden Grove-st., James McGinnis was sentenced to seven days hard labour and John Smith was discharged. WOMEN'S SQUABBLE.—Emma Holmes was charged with using threats against Mary Lawless on April Bth, and Mary Lawless was charged on a cross-summons with assaulting Mrs. Holmes on the same date. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared for Mrs. Lawless, and Mr. Percy Davies for Mrs. Holmes. Mr. Browne said that the parties lived on the opposite sides of Orford-st. On Saturday morning Mrs. Lawless went into the house of a neighbour named Mrs. Swindells to ask her to go with her to the magistrates clerk's office, to get a summons against Mrs. Holmes' daughter. While in the house, Mrs. Holmes came in and used the threats complained of. During the row, Mrs. Lawless threw some bread at Mrs. Holmes, which missed her and hit the wall. That was the only assault for which Mrs. Lawless was summoned. Mrs. Lawless and several witnesses gave evidence, after which Mr. Davies, who said that what Mrs. Lawless had thrown at his client had made a wound in her forehead, thought that the case ought to drop, and the two parties shake hands, to which Mr. Browne agreed. Amid loud laughter in the court, Mrs. Lawless and Mrs. Holmes were requested by their solicitors to shake hands. Mrs. Holmes said she was willing but Mrs. Lawless refused, much to the dm- gust of Mr. Browne, and the case was proceeded with, and resulted in both parties being bound over to keep the peace for six months, and each to pay their Olin Costs. APPLICATIONS FOR MAINTENANCE ORDERS.—Mr. Shearer applied for an order for is. 6d. each on Thomas and William Green for the maintenance of their father • is. per week on Wm. Cartwright, for his mother ; and for 4s. ad. per week on Robert Siddeley for his wife. The applications were granted. SMASHING WlNDOWS.—Patrick Burke was charged with breaking the glass windows of the house of Mary Thornton, Dial-street, on April 13th, and doing damage to the extent of £1 10s. Mary Thornton stated that de- fendant with two others was standing at the outside of her house at locking up time on Thursday. When she bolted the door, defendant commenced smashing the windows and broke every window pane at the front of the house. D. S. Monoghan corroborated. Defendant was fined 10s. and costs, and was ordered to pay the damage of the windows, or in default one month's hard labour. COLLECTING ON HIS OWN ACCOUNT.—At the Warring- ton Police Court on Friday, a young man named Geo. Wm. Dixon, was charged that, on March 25th he by false pretences, obtained certain money from Lydia Shelmerdine, 1, Birchall-street. Mr. Arthur Browne, who appeared to prosecute, said that defendant was formerly employed by the Provident Cloth'ng Club, of which he was a collector until March 17th. ie of the persons from whom he oollected was Mrs. Shelmerdine. The objects of the club were as follows :—A person joins the club, and has shares in it for which he pays is. per share,per week,and when he has paid 83,he is entitled to a check for £l, and still goes on paying is. per week until the £l. is paid. Defendant was discharged by Mr. Know les, the Warrington agent, on March 17th,and on the follow- ing week, he did not collect. On the week after that, however, he went to Mrs. Skelmerdine's house as though he was a collector. She asked him where he had been the previous week, and he replied that he had been away from home. Mrs. Shelmerdine then paid him and took some fresh shares, the money paid down being Us. 6d. for which he gave her a receipt,as be said he had not brought his books, but promising to enter the money in them the following week. Nothing further was heard of hint until Mr. Knowles heard of the affair, and the police were informed, and prisoner was arrested in Newcastle. The case was adjourned until Tuesday next.

CARD PLAYING !teem—Thomas Holden was charged with gaming with cards at the Stenehills, on Sunday afternoon, and with assaulting P.C. Gregory while in the execution of his duty.—P.C.'s Jewett and Gregory proved the gambling. The prisoner had struck Gregory twice on the chest.—Superintendent Atherton described this card playing as an abominable nuisance, and said he had to put plain clothes men on to catchthe offenders —The Bench expressed their determination to put down the practice and fined the prisoner 53. and costs 83. for the gaming and 10s. and costs for the assault, or in de- fault 21 days. TRH HALTON BLACEsMITH AND HIS WlFE.—Frederick Ellison. blacksmith, Stretford, was summoned by his wife. Ellen Ellison. of Halton. for deserting her. Mr. E. Wynne Humphreys appeared for complainant, and Mr. liatty, solicitor, Manchester, represented defendant. —Mr. Humphreys stated that the parties unfortunately had been married 33 years, and had four children, all grown up. Up to four years ago, they resided at Nor- ton, where the defendant was in the employ of Sir Richard M. Brooke, but was now engaged at Stretford. In May 1889, he took the Railway Vaults, Nantwich,and his wife managed it. Be continued at Norton until the succeeding February, but on several occasions went over and assaulted her, knocking four teeth out on oRe occasion. In February 1890, he went to the Vaults, and on being asked to assist in the house he refused. He was continually in drink, and if he served a customer, he took good care to spend the money. He went to Crewe, and thence to Stretford, where he had lived for two years. She asked for a home, when he said there was.a cellar which was good enough for her. • He had insinuated that his wife had committed adultery, where- as he himself had been guilty of misconduct. He had gone so far as to propose marriage to another woman, under the belief that he was a widower.—Ellen Ellison having given evidence in support of theabove statement said in cross-examination that it was not a fact that the summons had been taken out for annoyance. She had stated that she would not live with him again. She had not assaulted her husband, but had slapped him in the face three or four times. (Laughter.) Mi . Battystated that matrimonial difficulties were always very distressing There had been faults on both sides—fits of passion and abusive language on both sides, which had resulted in considerable unhappiness. He contended that there bad been no wilful desertion on the husband's part—in fact, the wife had deserted him. His client would deny the accusation of adultery. The Bench suggested that an effort should be made to come to an amicable settlement parties and their solicitors then retired. On return- tlg to court Mr. Humphreys said they were no nearer dement, but hoping time might bring about a change, they suggested an adjournment for a fortnight. It in the meantime they could agree to terms the Bench would hear nothing further of it.

From the drop out James Bate ran down to the Runcorn quar- ters, but he could not pass flou,ghton, and although Warrington early pressed the quick forward play of the homesters, caused the game to be of an even nature. Every inch of ground was vigorously contested, whilst a dribble by James Bate, Jolley, and Randlws saw Crompton baffled, but not so with Houghton, who met the opposing force in remarkably cool style, and another powerful kick drove the visitors back to neutral quar- ters. A capital display of passing by J. Faulkner and W. Faulkner saw Warder failing to take an easy chance. The miss led up to Warrington rushing past the half way, Holmes and Bandies leading the way, and a penalty falling to Fair Terner,the kick was a poor one, and Frank Turner following suit a moment later, a scramble ensued, Heesom stepping in the breach and forcing the oval at the home quarter. Houghton was tackled near his goal, and John Bate experienced the hardest of luck, actually getting over, but superior strength on the part of Houghton carried him back in play. A rush of the home forwards, headed by Farmer and Davies,was followed by splendid play on the part of Houghton and Boscow;the Warringtonian this time hav- ing the better of the exchanges. Evans now shone with a good run along touch, and finally punted to Houghton, but again the Runcorn defence was sound. The Warringtonians so far had had the best of this half, and it was now Runcorn's turn to attack for a time. James Bate kicking over Houghton's head, and Evans raced up for the try, but a plea of being touch in goal was sustained. From the drop out Warrington still pursued their aggressive work, and it again fell to the lot of Houghton to clear, a brilliant run by that player being marred by his pass being forward. The visitors would not be denied, and in the last few minutes Barnes essayed a drop at goal, but the shot being wide, Evans raced up and dodging his opponents got in be- hind the posts, but again was the point overruled. The rest of the play was confined to half way, and the finish of an exciting game was a Victory for Runcorn by a try and a minor to three mieors. The game, if lacking in finish, was nevertheless exciting. Both sides were at fault in many of their essays, especially so when near the line. In close rushing movements Warrington held a distinct advant- age, but to the winners praise is due for the admirable way they followed up the powerful punts of their custodian. By this means Boscow was severely harassed and the wonder was that Runcorn were kept at bay in the early stages of the game. Towards the close the homesters were kept strictly on the defensive, and it must be admitted that Warrington experienced the hardest luck. Individually Houghton stood out as being the best back on the field. Not only were his powerful punts of immense service to his side, but the occasional sprints he obliged with,raised the enthusiasm to a high pitch. Next to Houghton came J. Faulkner, and of the forwards Riley and Langley were the pick. On the Warrington side, Heesom had the most to do of the three-quarters, and this was accomplished in a finished style. The two Bates got through a vast amount of work with credit, and of the forwards none did better than Holmes, Berry, and Jolley. It is to be regretted that Abrams met with an accident which com- pelled him to retire in the second half, whilst Jolley was also asked to retire for adopting too vigorous tactics.

Faulkner scoring a try, Warder failing at goal. After this the Widnes defence had a warm time of it. Time after time Brennan had to clear his lines, and was ably supported by Barber and Wright. ASSOCIATION. MEDAL COMPETITION.—SECOND ROUND. WARRINGTON CRICKET v. ST. ELPRINS.—These teams met on Saturday, on the Cricket ground, Arley, to which of them should enter the setni-ffinal for this competition. There wese about a thousama specta- tors present. St. Elphins won the toss and defended the east goal. Alderman Francomb, in the absence of Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P., kicked off. The Cricket Club at onoe made tracks for the Saints' goal, but were repulsed by Marshall and the Rev. T. W. Cook. The Saints now had their turn, and J. Colley missed two splendid chances of scoring by shooting too high. From the goal kick, the Cricket men ran across the field, and Cook saved just in the nick of time. The Saints again attacked and Fc saved a stinger from Clayton. A good centre was made by Itherwood,and the Saints had great difficulty in clear- ing, but the ball was at length shot wide. From the goal kick, the Saints rushed the ball across the field, and an exciting scrimmage took place before the Cricket men's goal, and at length Scarisbrick scored. Imme- diately after the re-start, the Saints pressed again, and from a penalty kick Forrest had to concede a corner, which, however, was fruitless. Connor had hard lines in not scoring, Marshall spoiling him. Exciting play followed, the ball bobbing in front of the Saints' goal from time to time. Half time—St. Elphins, one goal ; Cricket, nil. The Cricket Club had the best of matters for some time, when Marshall was ordered off the field for rough play, and the Cricket equalised from the penalty kick given. St.Elphins' chance seemed hopeless now, as they had to play the remainder of the game with only ten men against the wind, but they played up pluckily,and Higginbottom again placed them ahead. The Rev. T. W. Cook at this portion of the game per- formed wonders, and prevented the Cricket from scoring repeatedly, but at length Connor got past Bates. Each team now put forward their utmost to gain the winning point,and about hall-a-minute from time Clayton scored from a scrimmage, St. Elphins thus winning by 3 goals to Cricket 2 goals. RUGBY. LANCASHIRE CUP COMPETITION. WARRINGTON v. SWINTON. The return fixture between these clubs stood out as being the most important in Lancashire Rugby football on Saturday. The match was played en the spacious ground at Wilderspool, and the fact that Swinton are leaders in this year's League, coupled with the know- ledge that the homesters were determined on wiping out that two goals defeat incurred at the early part of the season, there was no wonder that the " gate ' was a large ene—the second largest of the season. Swinton were fresh from their tour in Wales, and their combina- tion included that capital player, F. P. Jones the New Brighton and International three-quarter. Originally the visitors intended to play the Welsh system of four three-quarters, but the absence of Winterbottom saw the visitors adopting the orthodox style, and Lewis was drafted to back, and a place found for C. Honey in the forwards. The homesters were without the capable services of Ashton, Frank Turner, and Thorniley. It was ten minutes after the advertised time to kick-off- 3-30—before Bumby started towards the pavilion goal, and Swinton en the onset pressed. Kicks by Turner and James Bate changed operations to half-way, whilst exchanges between Bum- by and Evans saw the latter punting high to Valentine, and this player mulling, be was floored by Higham, Holmes, and Jolley, and Warrington set up a permanent attack. A rush by Jolley, Nevins, Berry, and Holmes was well met, and though Jones cleared the homesters would not be denied the invasion, and play was confined to the Swinton 25. Twice Valentine essayed to clear, but it was no go, and eventually a pretty bit of play by Eleesom completely beat Jones, and the oval being loose John Bate fielded well, and in five minutes time had

Crossed the Line, a noint which Fair. furner failed to improve. A mark to Boscow saw Evans tackling Pearson on his lines, and James Bate essayed a drop at goal, a minor being sent up. Following some brilliant play by the home forwards Lewis had a kick charged,but grand passing by Bumby, Brockbank, Valentine, and Jones saw the half-way line being crossed, and though Donohue gained a matter of ten yards in one determined struggle, another beautiful hit of passing started by Brockbank and taken up by Bumby, Valentine and Pearson ended in the latter Scoring at the Corner, the kick failing. With the teams on an equal footing the game was renewed, and a free kick to Turner saw Valentine again at fault. John Bate kicked well, whilst the play of Heesom was of such a nature as to win the admiration of a critical crowd. Sharpies and Hallam led the Manchester men out of danger, but Warrington persisted in the attack, and tricky play by Barnes and Nevins again saw Lewis conceding a miner. James Bate tried to get through, and Warrington being penal- ized, Holmes charged tke kick, which Valentine luckily recovered. RUNCORN V. WARRINGTON. This match was played at Runcorn on Wednesday evening before a large attendance. The clubs had not met in club fixtures for nearly seven years, and when it was decided at the commencement of the present season to renew them, satisfaction was expressed on all sides. It is satisfactory to note that the return will be played on the 24'h inst. Runcorn were without Morris and Kingston, but Abrams made a welcome re-appearance in tne team. SEQUEL TO A CROFT BREAra OF PROMISE CASE.

AN AGED DEFENDANT AT THE COUNTY COURT. AN AMUSING CASE.

A considerable amount of interest was taken in a case which came before his Honour,Judge Ffoulkes, at the Warrington County Court on Thursday. The case consisted of an application for an order for the recovery of £5O made by Mrs. Hannah Clough, widow, against Samuel Southern, farmer of Croft, the amount being due to the plaintiff under a verdict awarded her in a breach of promise case in which Southern was the defendant.—Mr. Jenkins appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. A. Browne for the defendant. Both the parties concerned have reached the age of 70. Before giving evidence the book was offered to the defendant to swear. He said : "No, I will hold my hand up, please."—The Judge: Very well then. Now listen to me. '1 swear by Almighty God." —Defendant: Yes.—The Judge: You must repeat what I said and raise your hand.—The Defendant All right sir. Start again please.—The defendant then ' repeated the oath correctly after his Honour. The first question he was asked after the oath was " What are you," and he answered "a labourer." Mr. Jenkins de- nied this and after a considerable amount of altercation it was proved that the defendant was the owner of two farms and some other property, but which Mr. Browne explained was "mortgaged up to the hilt " and the defendant realized nothing on it. Ile produced a cer- tificate from De_ Pattison, Newton, proving that the defendant was suff..ring from chronic rheumatism. He contended that the defendant had to work for his living and that his ailment prevented him from making winch more than 3s. ed. per week.—The Judge wanted to know if Mr. Jenkins' client would not be satisfied if the money was paid by instalments and not ask for a judgment summons.—Mr. Jenkins said he would be quite satisfied if the money was paid by instalments ; but Mr. Browne said his client was unable to pay the debt even by instalments. He was not earning more than 3s. 6d. per week on the average. Mr. Jenkins said he would be satisfied with an instalment of Ll per month, and Mr. Browne said his client could not pay it. The defendant here became somewhat over anxious in sup- porting this contention and taking out a piece of crumpled paper from his waistcoat pocket, he reached over to his Honour and said " This will prove, clear." His Honour did not stretch out for the paper in response so the defendant said " Howd on a minute I'll bring it round." He then got out of the box and commenced to climb up towards the judge's chair; but an officer of the court with great concern rushed from the other side of the court and put an end to the well meant attempt to faciliate business. Defendant then commenced to expos- tulate freely but was told to "hold yourtongue 'by the judge. Alter a considerable amount of haranguing, the plaintiff said she would accept 14s. per week. The defendant said : " Pooh ! Pooh I Couldn't be done." But Mr. Jenkins persisted that his client would not ac- cept anything less. Mr. Browne offered 3s. fid. per month ; but his Honour eventually made an order for the payment of the debt by monthly instalments of 14s.

===== 0002601_18930415_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
FUNERAL OF THE LITE MR. W. BARLOW.

The interment of the late Mr. William Barlow, manager of the Warrington branch of the Liverpool and District Bank, took place at the cemetery on Monday, before a large gathering of friends and relatives. The cortege left the house of the deceased gentleman at twelve o'clock, preceded by a carriage containing the Revs. J. Kirtlan (superintendent of the Warrington Wesleyan circuit), A. Freeman, and T. Ross, who preformed the funeral rites. A number of the Wesleyan body and other friends walked in proces- sion as a mark of respect. There were about 21 carri- ages, nauf being private, those of private persons. The following were the chief mourners: Messrs. John Barlow (Thorn Lea, Wilmslow), William Barlow (Haw- thorn Grove, Wilmslow), Richard Barlow (Ramsbottom) W. Goldthorpe (Brook House, Burmage-lane, Levens- hulme), C. Fernihough (Southport), Samuel RigbV (Davenport, near Stockport), J. 0 Rigby (Romanhurst, Whit6combe, Gloucestershire), Mrs. J. Barlow, and Mrs. Rigby. There were also present,Major Pickmere,J.P.Drs. Smith,J.P.Cannell,and Sherratt,Mr.Brewer,Mr.Henshall, Mr. Alexander, Mrs. Garnett, Mr. David Garnett, Rev.J. Gunnell, Mr. Seddon, Mrs. Pickmere, Mr. John Rylands, J.P., Mr. E. J. Guest, J.P., Mr. John Fairclough, J.P., Mr. F. Monks. J.P., Mr. W. Long, J.P., Rev. Canon Dodd, Mr. J. Burgess, Mr. Ridgway, Alderman Chandley, Councillors Dixon, J. H. Fleming, Hepherd, Dakin, W. Tinsley, Mr. J L. Whittle (town clerk), Mr. John Fair- hurst (borough treasurer), Mr. H. Longdin (borough surveyor), Mr. R.Dale, Mr. W.Gleave, Mr. J.Gandy, Mr. Taylor, Mr. J. S. Garland, Mr. S. B. Stringer, Mr. E. Place, Mr. Isaac Hayward, Mr. John Hartley, Mr. G. Weaver, Mr. Worsley, Mr. W:. Silcock, Mr. W. Dun- bobbin, Mr. Nicholson, and representatives of various banks in the town. Many floral tributes had been sent by friends and relati ides. The funeral arrangements were in the hands of Messrs. R. Girnett and Sons, and Messrs. J. and W. Dutton respectively.

WARRINGTON EQUALIZED DISTRICT SOCIETY OF DRUIDS.

The quarterly meeting of the above district took place on Saturday, in the Infante,' Schoolroom, Warrington-road, Penketh, where the new lodge, Penketh United, bolds its meetings. The officers and members of the lodge decided to commemorate the first visit of the district by holding a procession, tea party, and entertainment. At about two o'clock, about 80 members marched in procession, led on by the Pen- keth Brass Ba,pd, along Warrington-road to meet the district officers and delegates, who journeyed from War- rington by 'bus. The procession was then re-formed and marshalled by P.D.G.M.G. Roughley, P.D.G.M.G. JBllev,and P.A.J. Tetlow. Banner-Master J. Booth and P.D.G.M. Simcock then unfurled the banner, the district officers and delegates taking their places behind it and the band. A start was then made in the following order:—D.G.M. S. Caldwell, D.D.G.M. S. Dunbobbin, D.T. Wm. Barlow, D.C.S. T. Itlbarts trustees and dele- gates, lodge officers and members, Win. Woods, N G.A. J Stringer, P.D.6.1V1.T. Eden, P.D.G.M. William Crosskill, lodge secretary ; members proceeding along Heath-road, Greystone-road, Warrington-road, Chapel- road, Station-road, thence to the schoolroom. The band acquitted themselves very creditably by playing lively airs along the route. At .5 30, 150 members sat down to an excellent tea, and after tea an entertainment was held, Dr. Murray (medical officer of the lodge) presiding. 'the Chairman, in his opening remarks, referred in eulo- gistic terms to the success of the lodge, stating that it was only three months since the inception of the lodge, and it now consisted of 80 members. lie had long ago thought there was need of a friendly society in that village, and the rapid growth of the lodge proved it. P.M.W.R.A. T. Roberts, D.C.S., then explained at length the objects of the society, and the worth of the various funds,the totals collectively amounting to a sum equal to £7,003. The following programme was then rendered in a creditable manner, and elicited great applause :—Song. "Hearts of oak," Mr. T.Swift ; recita- tion, "The lifeboat," Mr.T. Eden ; duet, violin and flute, Brothers Ray ; song, "Riding on the hay," Miss Moston ; Lancashire recitation, Mr. Maseey ; song, " True till death," Mr. Molyneux ; duet, violin anti flute, Brothers Ray ; song. "The anchor's weighed," Mr. Kent; recita- tion, Mr. Eden; song, "Wreath of Rlses," Miss Moston ; selection on piano, Mr. Craig; duet, violin and flute, Brothers Ray ; recitation, Mr.Poynton. Votes of thanks were heartily given to the artistes, also to the ladies and I committee, and to Dr. Murray for presiding. The dis- trict officers before separating recognised the services of itteN hand trz, vntinct a handanma donation to w fund. An enjoyable evening' was brought to a close at 9 30, amidst hearty congratulations of all for what is hoped will be a red letter day in the history of friendly societies connected with Penketh and the Warrington Equalized District Society of Druids.

MR. GLADSTONE AS A POET.

_ How few people know (writes the London correspon- dent of the" Glasgow Herald ") that Mr. Gladstone is a poet ; to be more exact, that he has written at least one very fine ode, full of solemn beauty, suggestive of inspiration by Milton, hut perhaps more akin in style to the best work of Mr. Coventry Patmore. I refer to his "Ode on an Infant who was Born, was Baptised, and Died on the Same Day." This poem has been recalled , to my memory by sight of a pamphlet that has reached me from abroad, with the strange title, "The Damna- tion of children." In it the author holds that no writer of eminence has expressed a conviction that a new-born child, if it die immediately, passes at once "into the full spiritual inheritance of the human soul." He is evidently unacquainted with Mr. Gladstone's beautiful ode, first printed more than twenty years ago, but written, I be- lieve, many years earlier. In this connection I may add that it is strange no one has seen the aptness to the pre- sent political crisis of the opening of the eleventh sec- tion of this ode We are amid the tumult and the stress Of a fierce eddying fight; And, to our moral sight, Our fate is tremblug in the balances.

IMPERIAL PARLIAM EN 1.1.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. MUNDELLA, replying to a question as to a statement made by Lord R. CHURCHILL at Liverpool last Friday to the effect that the Labour Bureau was established by 7.he late Tory Government, said the noble L ,rd had been entirely misinformed, the office in question having been created by himself (Mr. MUNDELLA). Lord R. CaoRCHILL said he quite recognised that he had made a great mis- take, and he apologised to the House for it. The debate on the motion for the second reading of the Home Rule Bill was resumed. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN said the authors of the measure had to show that it would produce the effects they claimed for it, and had no right to call on their opponents to produce an alternative. He challenged the Nationalists to say that they accepted , the principle of the supremacy of the Imperial Parlia- ment its the sense in which it was accepted in England, and asked how a bill which left open 8,4 many questions was going to be a final settlement of the controversy. Mr. JUSTIN M'CareraY said that though the bill contained points of moment which they would strive to amend in Committee, he and his friends accepted it as an honest attempt at the settlement of the whole national question. Mr. W. REDMOND said he should vote for the second reading, but should wish the bill to be amended, in the financial clauses particularly. Sir G. TREVRLYAN after- wards spoke in the debate, which was adjourned at midnight.

TUESDAY. in the House of Common, on Tuesday evening, Mr. Asaurra informed Mr. Howell that everything possible was being done to effect the arrest of Mr. J. S. Balfour. Several questions were put in reference to the labour difficulties at Hull, and Mr. Asquith, in replying, said he had no power to order ths withdrawal of the military. The responsibility for calling in the aid of that force rested with the local authority. After a scene arising out of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S quotation on Monday night of Mr. Gladstone's reference some years ago to "disintegra- tion and dismemberment of the Empire "—in the course of which the Premier spoke of Mr. Chamberlain's action as offensive, and said he declined to associate anybody except Mr. Parnell with the doctrines he (Mr. Gladstone) was denouncing—the debate on the motion for the second reading of the Home Rule Bill was resumed. Mr. Davitt, while urging the necessity for amendment of some of ths clauses, said the frien is of peace in Ireland and Great Britain accepted the measure as containing all the terms and conditions of an honourable and lasting compact. Be was strongly in favour of the retention of the Irish members at Westminster. WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons, on Wednesday Mr. Asciurrxt informed Mr.KRIA HARDIE that he had requested the local authorities to report to him what was, under the present circumstances, the necessity for the continued presence of the military and naval forces of the Crown at Hull. Mr. KEIR HARDIE then asked leave to move the adjourn- ment of the House in order to call attention to this sub- juct as a matter of urgent public importance, but only eight members stood up to support the motion. The debate on the Home Rule Sill was resumed by Mr. Aunaoss, who argued that the Irish Parliament was certain to use the plwer which it possessed in order to establish the complete independence of Ireland. Mr. Roundel', Mr. Hogan, Mr. Loder, Mr. E. .1. C. Morton, Mr. A. G. Murray, and Mr. Storey followed, and the latter was speaking in support of the measure when the debate was adjourned. The Weights and Measures Bill, was read a second time, and a bill, introdund by Sir Albert Rollit, for the amendment of the law of libel was read a first time.

". BUDDHISM AND til.R/STIANITY : SOME YOINTS OF RESEMBLANCE. Sir,—l preeuineMr. Dignum has now finished, and I wish to call attention to a few facts in conclusion. I have no desire to prolong the discussion which has now extended to considerable length, and although I do not in any degree' subscliibe to Mr. Diginun's contentions, and although.;,l take ~strong exception to the offensive personalities he euht se to indulge in, I am yet personally thankful to him for having , entered the lists with me, and thus been the means of spreading a know- ledge of this very,interesting.subject. ,:But where have the parsons been ?" They wha are supposing to be the leaders if the faithful,and the guardians of the faith have been conspicuous by their absence and their silence. Is it that they are ignorant of the subject, or are they too lazy to enlighten their people by a newspaper correspon- dence? Too many of them, alas ! find a r.-ore congenial place behind a protected pulpit—" coward castle" it has been aptly called—where no man can reply to them. 14 honour Mr. Dignum for his fearless advocacy of what deems to be right. I wonder how many parsons in .rrington can claim to havean acquaintance with Max eller and the other writers quoted by Mr. Dignum 21 ir not many. In my last letter I said I would deal with the scientific end philosophical aspect of both Buddhism and Chris- tianity, but I hardly care to carry the discussion any further. I will just say, however, that from a scientific point of view I am entirely in agreement with Max Muller that Christianity is a development of religions that have existed long anterior to it—that it is in fact another instance of the great law of Evolution The bible itself is another instance of the same eternal law. It is self-evident to any one who has eyes tc see and a mind to observe that this is so. They will find in the early pages of the bible that the writers had but very elementary ideas of morality—ideas positively shocking Loom. more highly developed minds. Their conceptions of God were of a very childish character. He was depicted as a sort of magnified man who walked and talked in the Garden of Eden with Adam, and who was of a violent and bleed-thirsty character, guilty of the most abominable crimes—" crimes that a respectable devil would be ashamed of." This is the language of an eminent minister of Liverpool. Further on in the bible we find abundant evidence of development, and the writer speak of,eriod not as a huge man but as a Spirit siidl loirfng' Father. The prophet Micah expresses in very few words the very essence of true religion hen he says " What doth the Lord require of thee but to do aW' to love mercy, and to walk humbly with thy God." Now from a philosophical point of view which reli- gion* tliej beat Badtlbism or Christianity ? The Bud- dhists don't 'believe in a future life, the Christians do. The Buddhists believe that when a weary, sorrow- fitrjahitM mortal 'cliert he falls into an eternal sleep. "Christians believes that he lives after death. Now what evidenFe have the Christians of this ? Absolutely none whatitegi fl 1a a belief utterly incapable of verification. The only people who profess to verify it are the ,Spiritual4sts, ancl the mildest term that Christians ap- ply An chew, people is that of imposters. Then the Christians saf that Christ rose from the dead. Touching the Resurrection, Professor Fronde says that "our Lord seems purposely to have witheld such public proof of al as would have left no room for un- wed Himself" not to all the people," not whom His appearance would have over- to witnesses chosen before ; to the in friends. There was no evidence that a Lit that he was ever actually dead. So for persons crucified to die so soon, that Pilate, we are told, " marvelled." The subsequent appearances were strange, and scarcely intelligible. Those who eaw Him did not recognise Him till He was pad? ,them by the breaking of bread. He was vist anti ILlVltlivic. tie was wbo were most intimate with Him for another person ; ,ndrsiu theaccounts agree which are given by the differ- tngelistß. Of investigation in the modern sense (except in the one instance of St. Thomas, and St. Tkontasoviasi rather rebuked than p-aised) there was .tiotie anti could be none. The evidence offered was different in kind, and the blessing was not to those who sfalisfitid themselves of the truth ef the fact by a search- ' ?rig inetuiry, but who gave their assent with the unhesi- tating confidence of love." It must be remembered that these are not the words of either a Spiritualist or an Athiest, but of a professor at Oxford University. Ac- cording to Christianity, men live after death, and a ,szwll, percentage (only Christians of course) pass into 4flthe rest ol mankind are doomed to eternal torment. The heaven of Christians is a big jewellers' shop—a place that no sane man would care to enter. si!hatnert of a place is hell, the place to which a loving Fattier is said to consign countless millions of His cr.atures Listen to the words of a peat Christian preacher, the Wel Mr. Spurgeon—" There is a place as much beneath imagination as heaven is above it, a place of murky darkness, where only lurid flames make darkness visible splace,where beds of flame are the fearful couches upon which spirits groan, a place where God Almighty from His mouth pours a stream of brimstone, kiAlingdthat c Thu Gi noetdi htahse rpereitzraedspooftowl hose I as tge /Ili, re bt the wood los; awnhdi only sights are scenes of fearful woe. There is a place Iclolnr\t know where it is, somewhere—not in the bowels bt the earth, I trust, for that were a sad thing for this world to have hell within its bowels—but somewhere in a far-off world there is a place where the only music is the mournful symphony of damned spirits ; where howling, groaning, moaning, wailing, and gnashing of ,terth„ Take up the horrid concert. There is a place *lire demons fly swift as air, with whips of knotted burning wire torturing poor souls ; where tongues on fire with agony burn the roofs of mouths that shriek for drops of water—that water all denied. There is a place where soul and body endure as much of infinite wrath as thealnite can hear, where the inflictions of justice crush ttf4lll, where the continual ftagellations of vengence beat 'the+ flesh, where the perpetual pourings out of eternal wrath scald the spirit, and where the cuttings of the sword strike deep into the inner man." Sir, better a thousand times the ".ghastly Nirvana," the eternal sleep of the Bueldhists,than such a life eternal as this. I wonder Christians are not heartily ashamed of such a revolting creed. THE EXAMINER, s TITRD kY, APRIL 15, 1893.

devilish than the foulest devil. (See Rev. Mr. Aked's sermon on hell.) In conclusion I re-affirm all my former statements as to the remarkable resemblances between Buddhism and Christianity, and Mr. Dignum has not disproved one of them. My statements have been amply authenticated by his own authority Max Muller, and also by the Encyclopedia Brittannica ; and also by Archdeacon Wilson. As to his statements about the suuess of Christian missions in India I repudiate them absolutely. The statements emanate from interested parties and are quite at variance with the statements I made by many Christian ministers. I once heard a I Congregational minister say that he hoped he would never be guilty of the sin of giving a single copper to Christian missions. The statements of Mr. W. S. Caine, who visited India,will recur to many who read his letters which appeared in several journals. It must also be remembered that the natives of India are very poor and supplies of food and clothing have had much to du with some of their conversions. Further, that it is customary to include as converts those who attend the schools established by the missionaries. I will now leave the matter with your readers to form their own opinions upon the subject. Thanking you for your kindness, I am, yours truly.

the!Orangemm, and all the objections raised by bigotry and privilege ;in spite of objections raised by others which are more entitled to our respect, has been proved, Point by point, so far as facts and fair inference can prove anything, to be hopelessly in the wrong. I should just like in conclusion to thank gentlemen holding opposite views to my own for the invariable courtesy received from them both inside and outside the Debating Society. This goes to show that it is still pos- sible even for politicians (when not unduly excited), to thrash out the questions of the day in the spirit in which Carlyle and John Sterling conducted their arguments ; that is to say, in the words of the former, " except in opinon not differing." With apologies for the rather inordinate length of this letter.—l remain, yours truly,

A. H. CROSFIELD.
