HARRY ESMOND.

"THE NEW LIBERALISM."

Sir,—The paper on "New Liberalism " which you were good enough to print in extenso, has been made the subject of various comments which I may divide int* three classes.-Ist. The remarks of those who on the whole agree with it.-2ud. What in Mr. Gladstone's recent phrase may be described as " unfavourable homilies."-3rd. That sort of scurrilous impudence which I suppose is only to be expected in certain quarters, and which uniting stupid and misleading fallacies with vulgar personalities, deserves to be treated, and, so far as I am concerned, invariably will be treated with silent contempt. But I should like to say 'a word as to these unfavourable homilies received both viva voce and by letter. For example, a Liberal Unionist friend of mine writes that my paper is calculated to set class against class, and is dangerous, more particularly in its advo- cacy of a reduction of royalties and licenses without compensation. Well,in the first place as regards royalties, let us on the authority of, say, Mr. Burt or such a reliable source of information as the "Manchester Guardian," assume that excessive royalties may and do impede the development ,of some of our greatest industries. For instance, it is tolerably plain to anyone, that coal seams may not be opened up through fear that the land-owner's royalty may swallow up any probable profit that might result from the enterprise; and again, that businesses which might be just "on the margin of cultivation" may be just not worth cultivating. The account I quoted of Barrow is a case in point. It is beyond question that ex- cessive royalties bad been a factor of paramount impor- tance in producing that extreme paralysis of trade. Then I ask these questions. (a). Is the reform of such a condition of things, or its existence, most likely to set , class against class? (b). Are land-owners justified in charging excessive royalties ? and, if not. (c). Why are we to pay compensation for reduction to reasonable figures. On the whole, I consider this a not unfitting example of one of those cases for reform generally ignored by the Tories until the Liberals either force their hand, or un- dertake the business themselves. Choosing it as iamb, I notwithstanding wrote, or intended to write, with studied indefiniteness, as to the scope and method of the I reform ; for I am not wholly unacquainted with the complexities of the question, and am perfectly well ' aware that it is one in the settlement of which we need to tap the brains of specialists, as has been done under the commission which has just issued its interesting re- port, and as it may be necess try further to do so. I may just add that the problem of royalties and wayleaves, like other hindrances to trade, is not to be solved by Protection, as indeed the intelligent section of the Con- servative party recognise, and is even a trifle beyond the brains of a fifth rate party hack. Next as to compensation for the extinction ef licenses. In answer to a question the other day as to whether his party really meant to stand by Mr. Goschen's scheme of compensation, as a condition for any improvement of the existing system, a leading Conservative frankly replied "you cannot compensate." Compensation for the working publican, there may be. The plea for that is, I take it, pretty well recognised by the Liberal party; and, as to compensation to cover the expenses of turning a public-house into a shop, accepted for instance by Mr. Brunner. We shall therefore not stick at that. But the all important question is, whether we are to accept this scheme of Mr. Goschen, annihilated in the last Parlia- ment. but now being put forward again and adopted by Mr. Chamberlain. On this point, our minds are fully made up. We won't accept the policy which Mr. Glad- stone forcibly described as the endowment of public- houses, and according to whicti the owner of a house that, as it stands, might he worth £2OO, but with its license becomes worth £2,000, is entitled to full compen- sation at this market price. All things considered, we are of opinion, that we neither can nor ought to allow it. So much for that part of my paper, which is said to be dangerous. . . . . _

' My Unionist friend writes further, "lam free to con- fess, that I grow more Conservative as time goes on,and perhaps 20 years hence, you will be the same." Well in one and the best sense of the word, I hope to prove my- selt Conservative a little sooner than that. _ _ _

The leader of the Opposition remarked the other day that whenever Mr. Gladstone wants to pay a politician a compliment, he calls him a Conservative. Precisely. Conservatism owes its existence to Liberalism. But, unfortunately, it constantly forgets, or ignores, the laws of its existence. As bearing on that, I confess that some of these friendly remonstrances remind me very forci- bly of a passage in Emerson's "English Traits." The great American, of rer paying our National character a high tribute, one which any Englishman may value,and not least for its absolute honesty—indulges towards the close in one of his bold and vivid metaphors. He says " There is cramp limitation in their habit of thought, sleepy routine, and a tortoise's instinct to hold hard to the ground with his claws, lest he should be thrown on his back. There is a drag of inertia which resistu reform in every shape ;—law-reform, army reform, extension of suffrage Jewish franchise, Catholic etnanci • pation, the abolition of slavery, of impressment, penal code and entails.* They praise this drag, under the . formula that it is the excellence of the British constitu- tion, that no law can anticipate the public opinion. These poor tortoise's must hold hard, for they feel no wings sprouting at their shoulders. Yet somewhat divine warms at their heart and waits a happier hour. It hides in their study will," &c., Sce. This is perhaps not altogether complimentary. But I quote it as having struck me in its connection with the I tone of my Conservative and Unionist friend's com- ments. Now tenacity is one of the qualities on which wa pride °unwires, and is a very good thing; but blind tenacity, the sort which clutches at everything.4oo4 and bad together, is net. Therefore the Liberal party has to undertake the periodic and chronic duty of opening the eyes of our sleepy Tory tortoise. We can- not exactly promise it wings and we may at least I trust that it will not frequently be necessary to apply that process, which Mr. Courtney briefly, and, as some of his Conservative friends may perhaps have thought,rather brutally, described as prodding. But we must at least endeavour to develop some latent germs of elasticity in the animal. I could name a score of instances in which this gentle stimulation has been and will be necessary, but I do not wish to prolong this' letter further, except by saying a word or two to my unknown friend " Liberal." He will excuse me correct- ing an erroneous assumption which is apparently im- plied in the last lines of his letter, in view of which cor- rection,he may probably take less interest in my opinions. , But if by chance they el° still interest him at all, I hate LIO6 "la Lic.BII.6LIULI points on which Ido not agree with the policy of the present Government are—their method of attacking the drink question, and their attitude with regard to the next Brussels conference ; also that no one ha a the slightest pretext, or right to assume that I do not agree on any other. I cannot attempt to go into detail on these two large questions, but, as regards currency, I may just remark that the hard and last and high and mighty opinions of monometallists are apparently undergoing some rnodi- cation which will pave the way for a settlement of this abstruse but vitally important question on one or other of the several lines proposed as alternatives to a statu quo which is simply intolerable. As to drink, in one word, some of us prefer practicable temperance to impracticable prohibition. Tempting as is the subject I must leave it, and iccleed at the present moment one is little inclined to air points of difference that can be settled later on. Despite divergence of opinion on cer- tain subjects, there has seldom been a finer example of energetic and unibd work on the Liberal side than in the present Parliament. Seldom, one may safely add, has there been a sore powerful body of men, of real earnestness at theaead of affairs. And the history of the world itself miy almost fail to find a parallel to that sublime epectacleof constancy and true heroism which we have in the ared minister at their head, who, old as he is, still endure an amount of labour arid anxiety, carries, so to spfak, a weight of armour in this tremen- dous battle, ulster which strong manly od itself might faint. That exanple of itself makes the pulse of Liberal- ism throb thwughout the length and breadth of the land. If then is united effort inside Parliament, we on the other hard, whose work lies outside, join our ranks and accumukte the power of public opinion, with the force of whioh behind him our old chief is enatled to encounter mt merely the dead weight of ordinary Tory- ism, but tle wretched prejudices and mean hatred aroused orthe Irish question; and last, but not least, the maliglitY of his bitter foe from Birmingham, and that both of more sincere Unionists which unfortunately follow be leadership. NOTES ON GAMES AND

A rHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

--- Downright determination to do his best to win the game himself, was what characterised every player engaged in the match between Warrington and Swinton, on Saturday. If one could describe players of football as " going at it hammer and tongs," then I say an ambition to do that, got up and walked about in the breast of every player. Well, maybe not every player, but almost so. Jones, the international player, graced the ranks of Swinton, but he didn't seem to be inflated with much superfluous ability—didn't seem to have very much more than the P.D. himself did when he used to land goals over " ma's" props with " pa's " silk hat. But, then, as the "Athletic News " man says " his re- putation preceded him—and got lost in the crowd."

The match was certainly one of the most interesting of the season. Swinton are leading the scores in that competition and have long since been adjudged by some people to be the future holders of the cup. Warrington have lately been out of form somehow, and consequently it was rather expected that they would get knocked about a bit. And Swinton were assured that they would find Warrington quite ready to lie at their feet and be kicked ; and provide lemons into the bargain. But did they ? Not quite ;it is a matter beyGnd doubt what difference there was between the two teams, was in favour ef Warrington.

Boscow was slightly off form but I was glad to see ou.t old friend Barnes out again on what might almost be termed a '6 state occasion." Although not doing any- thing big, he showed some of his old form.

Although the matches in connecti-,n with the Warring- ton Junior League Competition are not long draws so far as gates are concerned, they are very much so in the way of play. Another draw was recorded on Saturday in the match between the Parish Church and Latchford.

ASSOCIAT/ON.

Although St. Elphins won the match which took place between them and W.A.C.C. the game was not altogether a matter of apple pie and nuts for them. The fast of the almost equal merits of the teams at- tracted one of the largest "gates" that Association matches have been honoured with in Warrington. Such a "gate" had not probably been anticipated, for no preparations had been made to deal with a rush. Con- sequently, when 100 persons all tried to get through a small gateway which will only admit one at ditime,there " was a sound of cracking ribs." This want of prepara- tion was a very absurd oversight on the part of the com- mittee, and received a considerable amount of reprehen- sion from the spectators.

The match was marred by one unpleasant incident, and that was that one of the St. Elphins full backs was ordered off the field by the referee. This was very regrettable, more especially as it came from such a team as St. Elphins ! I hope the individual concerned will take it as a lesson and not allow his zeal to overcome his judgment in future.

The semi-final in the medal competition will be played to-day on the cricket ground, and the Committee intend to make a " boom " over it. St. Elphins and Orford Barracks will meet and play under the soothing influences of Orford Barracks band, who are going to play by the permission of the officers. I like the idea, somehow. Music is said to have charms "to sooth the savage breast ;"and whose breasts become MOM savage at times than that of the Knight of the Leather! St. Elphins, too, are bringing some clerical power to their aid. The Rev. T. W. Cook will play full back again, and the Rev. F. W. Willis, Rector of Warrington will kick off.

By the way. Jam glad N see that the Committee are advanced free traders in one branch of their commercial transactions.

MISCELLANEOTJS.

I notice that last week Thomas Green, of Warrington, won the 120 yards handicap on the Ardwick Cricket Ground. Green was trained by E. Walsh, ohm Tudsey, from the Strangers Arms.

CRICKET.

; Amongst the Australian cricketers who will shortly visit our shores there will be no 'herb familiar face and figure than that of Alexander Ckarles Bannerman. Good old stonewaller, patient as the sphinx, short of stature, tall in scoring abilities, he is one of the oldest men in the team, having been born in Sydney 34 years ago. He formed one of the first Australian elevens that visited this countryin 1878,and on four other occasions he has come to show us what Patience on a monument may do with a bat. tie has been known to make one run in ninety minutes, although his average rate of scoring is a trifle faster than that. At cover-point and mid-off he has the eye of a needle, and Is positively brilliant in either position.

One of the Australian cricket team of whom much is I expected with the bat is Henry Graham, of South Mel- bourne. He is one of the youngest members in the teams and has not visited England before. His average in inter-colonial matches this season is over fifty-six runs per innings. With a pretty style and just enough dash to play a safe game, and at the same time raise the enthu- siasm of the crowd, he is likely to become extremely popular during his visit to England.

As an understudy to Blackham, A. H. Tarvis, of the Australian cricket eleven, will fill an important position. When he visited England with the Australian teams of 'BB and 'BB he occasionally kept wicket, besides batting at times in excellent style. His inter-colonial average during the present season is twenty-nine runs per inn- ings, and he is said to have improved lately as a wicket- keeper. He stands up to the stumps to all sorts of bowl- ing, and takes the ball in either side with elegence and ease.

Who that knows anything of English or Australian cricket does not know the swarthy face, bearded like the pard, of John M'Carthy Blackham, the oldest M3M. ber of the Australian cricket team. He is a Melbourne matt, having been born there nearly thirty nine years ago. Although not an orator, he is strong on the stump, irelWTMUFtgldagoril,r:,;vltps,c Pro.,-u. Cornstalk who has been a member of every team that has visited England. It is a marvel to see him bat. He : is neither a Bannerman nor a Shrewsbury ; he believes ' all sorts of balls were made for hitting, and each time with him it is neck or nothing. By this method he oc- casionally makes a score with a figure before the noth• lug, but often enough it is missing.

William Bruce, who cornea over with the Australian cricketers, was not a consnicuous success when he visited England with the 1886 eleven, but since then he has developed into one of the most consistent and finished batsmen in Australia. 11e has a striking style in more senses than one, and like most lefthanded batsmen is strongest to his strokes to the off. When he visited England seven years ago, he did so on the strength of one big innings-328 not out. On the present occasion he comes as a finished bat, a fair bowler, and a smart field.

WHAT'S TO BE DONE?

BY MR. 11. WRITHAIL Readers of the "Examiner" seem to be in a fair way for knowing as much about the States, and the way there as those who have taken the actual risks. Crossing the turbulent track is, no doubt, an interesting feat, pleasurably or otherwise just as the finances decide. After landing, knowing where to locate—if this has not been decided before- hand—is all-important. If one has a few hundred dollars, and a love for farming, or other occupations connected with land, I should not hesitate for a moment to recommend the wild and woody west.

----- But even going west requires that the voyager should know himself, as well as the region he is going to. Here in New England, going to Chicago is going west. To keep in a north-westerly direction is to find the great grain griming states, and it is here where a man should know himself thoroughly. Brain and muscle and a sturdy deter- mination to hold one's own are requisites without which failure is pretty sure to overtake one. To keep in a southerly direction is to meet with a warmer or more equable climate, and the possioility of being able to sit beneath your own fig tree. As I have said, to the one with a little money, and lov- ing the land, it is no difficult matter to be very soon able to see your own orchard with its trees loaded with peaches, apricots, figs, and such like. When this is all accomplished it is very nice to write about, nice to read about, and makes one long, 'almost, to make the venture. But it is not my mission to write about such an Arcadian life. lam seeing and reading too much of a life that almost requires the pen to be dipped in gall, and can, therefore, only write with the word caution always in large letters before my eyes.

It is said that God made the country, and man made the town. I don't know how this may be; but, anyway, I think man's share of the work is not proving so satisfactory. Evidence is abundant that the towns are growing at the expense of the coun- try. Here, in the New England State of Massa- chussetts, the towns are fast becoming large and unwieldy centres of population, where overcrowd- ing and insanitary conditions are daily producing the same results as in the <Auer, and more densely populated cities of the world.

It seems to rae that the states lying near to the two great ports of New York and Boston are likely. in the near future, to find great trouw,le in either the over-population, er the quality of the population settling in them. At the very large works at which I find employment, it is a rare chance for me to run against a native born American. Swedes seem to be in the majority ; after them Irishmen, then Turks, Fins, and so on. The Swedes come to stay ; I suppose they make good citizens. All I know of them is that after they have once satisfied themselves that there is money in anything they go for it, night and flay, meal times and all times. The Turks, and Fins do rict as a rule, come to stay. To get a few hundred dols. is their ambition ; to achieve this they live, or pig together, ten or a dozen in one room. This saves rent, and enables them, after some five or six years, to return to their homes comparatively wealthy men. I find this state of things is going on through all the large iron manufacturing dis- tricts. Leaving these features of the labour ques- tion for a time let us look at another.

I have just read a book printed in Warrington, and written by a dreaming Warringtonian, and when I closed it I was led to exclaim 'and of such things are dreams made.' I know the writer to be of a kindly disposition, keen, and incisive, yet not one lo strike below the belt, still I felt all his dreamy references were so many travesties of his- tory, and all his dreaming only so much waste of good energy, trying to reach the impossible. I tried, and have long tried to think out this question of Federation—lmperial or otherwise,—but it was no use. With all my love for dear old England, I could not get anywhere near the practical side of the question, if it has one. Bellamy dreamed a dream, and the publication of it has done a deal of good, and so my friend, the above named writer, may have in his way done some good. I also would dream, but it should be of the possible; not as hav- ing to deal with men who must be only a little lower than the angels, but as dealing with men who seem to be only a little higher than the devils.

When I looked through my "Examiner" of March 4th, and read the account of the Appleton farmer's notice to quit, I can scarcely describe my feelings. Indignation was uppermost for a time, but this was soon replaced by a quiet feeling of satisfaction, to be followed with a sweet dream of peace. Before my mind's eye passed the Liberation Eociety, and the somewhat useless work it has for years been doing. Then I thought I saw the church itself, as established by law,—high church, low church, broad church, and narrow church,—riven a-id twisted out of all shape, each party striving for the mastery, until at last the Liberation Society stood on one side, smilingly allowing his Grace of Canterbury to bring in his Bill for disestablishing and disendowing the church of strife, and so bring peace.. Next I saw my Lord of Salisbury with a neat little bill under his arm, on the back of which I could lust discern the words, "A Bill to federate the people of the British Isles." His lordship was followed by a mighty gathering of Ulster men, with a sprinkling of what had been known as Liberal Unionists. I thought I saw amongst them,and look- ing somewhat ashamed, Colonel Saunderson, carry- ing a banner, on which were inscribed the wcrds of a good old legend, "Peace on earth, and goodwill towards all men." It seemed to me that when these questions were settled, as they soon were, for her Majesty's Opposition, led by Mr. Gladstone, loved their country and Lnew their duty, that at once a change came over the whole scene. The country squire was led to see that he and the parson, were but servants of the people; that land and people are inalienable, and the Arley squire was led to see that he could not own the land any more than he could own the people.

As these pleasing visions passed, methought I saw others of equal magnitude appear. Large work- , shops built.and arranged so that the workman pur- sued his daily avocation with pleasure; producing only what was needed, he seem'd to throw his whole soul into his work; and—what appeared, most strange to me—the managers were all flitting in and out amongst the workmen, not as so many Legrees,but as men having to deal with men,a, kind word here and a cheering smile there. Fault-finding and curses seemed to be all done away with, and the power of the "greatest thing of all; love" was brought to bear, and the effect was simply magical. Employers were no longer a class apart and distinct from the workmen, hut all had one common end in view,, the creation of a heaven here, upon earth. Alas, that this should all be a dream, and that the We travel in lands that never were known tinder the sky, And gather dream flowers that never have grown, My pen and I.

Recently, an American Senator, during a speech he was making, must have been having a sort of waking dream. He suddenly pulled himself up, and astonished the wondering Solons by abruptly' putting the question,"Where was I at?" lam just now in the same predicament. Where was I at? Two ideas were running together in my head and struggling to be set free—lmperial federation and emigration and labour. The first must have prece- dence in a future letter. We may not be able to answer the question, " What's to be done?' but we are bound to exchange ideas about it, 80 that the great work now going on shall be as satisfactory as we can make it to those who will follow after. Clarence-St., Worcester, Mass.

SUSPECTED POISONING OF A WOMEN AT WARRINGTON.

STRANGE REVELATIONS. --- A PATHETIC LETTER.

At the Board-room of the Workhouse, on Mon- day, Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, held the adjourned inquest on the body of Emily Passey,aged 28 years, who died in the Workhouse on the 18th of March.

===== 0002601_18930415_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY ANALYSTS' REPORT.

Mr. Brighouse said that the jury would recollect that the inquiry was adjourned for two objects, first because Dr. Spinks strongly suspected strychnine poisoning, and the contents were sent to the county analyst, to se whether there was any ground for Dr.Spink's statement, and secondly to see if they could possibly identify de- ceased. The analysis had been carried out by Dr. Camp- bell Brown, county analyst, and his colleague Dr. Collingwood Williams and the following was the certifi- cate sent by them :—" April 4th, 1893. We, the under- signed public analysts of the county of Lancashire, have submitted the stomach and contents, taken from the body of Emily Passey to careful analysis occupying 18 days, and we find no trace of strychnine in the same. There is nothing whatever in the appearance of the stomach that points to strychnine poisoning. There is no strychnine in it. Signed, Dr. Campbell Brown, Dr. Collingwood Williams." So far as the theory of poison- ing was concerned, there seemed to be an end of that suggestion. He thought that there would be some one in the world who would make some inquiry after de- ceased. The police of Warrington had searched and made inquiries in all directions to find out any of de- ceased's relatives, but so far they had been unable to find any of them. The only evidence they had of them was a letter found upon deceased, dated January 2nd, 1893 from Federalsburg, Maryland, America. The American Consul had been consulted, and he said there was a small village of that name in Maryland. The following is a copy of the letter :

===== 0002601_18930415_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PATHETIC LETTER.

"My Darling Sister,—Our Father in heaven only knows with what sorrow I read your letter a week ago. It must have been delayed on the road a long time. If I only had the money to send you to come right to me, but! haven't. Mother and us are both very slaort this winter, owing to the drought last summer spoiling the crops. We haven't had anything to sell, and had to sell a cow for want of food, and father had to sell all he had to pay the rent ; and as for Eliza, she could give you enough to pay your fare if she would . . . and Emma, don't ever think of going to her. I want when you .7et money, for you to come and make your home with me. Dear Sister, who is this Deighton, and what inftuencs can he have over you? In your letter was enclosed one from the matron. She said a gentle- man had written to me. I could not understand it, for' never received a letter from anyone but you. I can't understand in what place you are staying. God grant you are not in the condition she fears. Write, dear Sister, and tell me all when you get this, and don't ever think you are friendless. . . . Charlie and Ernest talk about you every day, and in their prayers at night they never forget Aunt Emma. . . . We all join in sending our best love to you, and remain your loving sister,Lizzix.—P.S.—Don't get discouraged. Remember that beautiful text "Acknowledge Him in all your thoughts and Ile will direct your paths."

===== 0002601_18930415_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
A STRANGE-MEETING.

Arthur Carter, who came to Duty's lodging-house, Scotland-road, with deceased on the night before her death, and was suspected of being concerned in her death, was then called. He said he was a joiner, resid- ing at 3, Keersley-street, Liverpool. On Wednesday night, the 18th ult., he was standing outside the British Workman Cocoa-rooms, Horsemarket-street, when deceased came up to him. She was crying. After a 'little conversation, she said, "It's very awkward for me. I can't get lodgings." Witness took pity on her and offered to pass himself off as her father, and get lodgings in Daly's lodging-house, to which she agreed. She gave witness fourpence for her lodging, I and they then went to the lodging-house. Next morn- ing, be saw that witness was very ill. She had told him the day before, that she had walked from Liverpool, and as be was going to Liverpool that day, he asked her if she had any message for any of her friends, but she said she had no message. Witness then left the house and walked to Liverpool. He never heard of the woman's death until informed by the police. He never saw her before he met her before the cocoa-rooms. He had been the head partner of the firm of Messrs. Carter and Martin, carters, Liverpool, and employed 350 men, but the firm broke, and he was glad to pick up jobs now. He was a freemason of the Lodge of Harmony, Ormskirk, which he joined on October 9, 1856. He lost his ticket when he was at Warrington, and made a report to the police, but had not found it yet. The coroner said that he had made inquiries of the secretary of the lodge at Ormskirk. and had found wit- ness's story to be correct. Tile jury thought witness's statement to be very straightforward.

===== 0002601_18930415_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CAUSE OF DEATFI.

Dr. Spinks was next called, and the coroner address- ing him said : " The poisoning theory seems to be dis- posed of by the result of the analysis.—Dr. Spinks : Completely so.—The coroner: As that is the case,are you able to give the j'iry any idea of the cause of the death of the woman I)—Dr. Spinks : Only that it is from con- vulsions arising from her condition.—The coroner : You found a tu:nour in the liver. Don't you think that had anything to do with the death ?—Dr. Spinks : No, and I don't think it would cause vomiting.—The Coroner: How large was it P—Dr. Spinks : It was as big as an apple.—The coroner: Quite big enough. I should not like one as big as a nut to be on my liver. (Laughter.) You, would say that death was due to natural causes.— Dr. Spinks : Yes.—The jury accordingly found that deceased had died from natural causes.—The coroner thought the police deserved great credit for their inquiry into the matter.

THE REV. MARK GUY PEARSE.

As this popular author and preacher is paying a visit to Warrington on Wednesday next, a few de- tails of his life and work will be interesting to those who wiah to hear him. Mark Guy Pearse was borne at Canaborne in Cornwall, on January 3rd 1812'. He states that his life was much influenced by the fact of having been born and bred in a town like Camborn. It being a small place, he had every opportunity of becoming intimately acquainted with various and striking types of character. There was room for leisurely growth of individuality,, and people thought their own thoughts in their own fashion. His schoolmaster was the Rev. W. W. Butlin, who is still Vicar ot Penpnunds, a man beloved and revered by his scholars, and who still finds a home for ministers when the Wesleyan conference is held at Camborne, but Mr. Pearsesa,ys it was from men like Dr. George Smith, of Trevu, and Captain Thomas' of the famous Dolcoath mine,that he learned his most important less ms, and came to unlerstand that the grandest thing in the world was to be a good man. As a boy he was brought into association with the fervent religious life of the Methodist people. Mr. Pearse mentions how, when a child, he was shocked on hearing that the Queen did not meet in class ! On leaving school he was articled to Dr. Elingston, of Liskeard, and began to prepare for the medical profession. In 1860 he came to London to eon- tinue his medical training at St. Bartholemew's Hospital, where, at the house of Mr. James Duncan, he met the Rev. Thomas Vasey, superintendent of the Hackney cir- cuit, who saw that he was called to another service,and in due course he entered the ministry. He had for some time previous to this, been a local preacher. His Intro- duction to this work was in a rather peculiar manner I The superintendent of the Liskeard circuit was unable to take an appointment at a village ten miles away. Mr. Pearse drove thither to inform the people, upon which the leader told him Ise would have to preach. It was in I vain to protest, and thus he preached his first sermon. After entering the Wesleyan ministry, he was stationed successively at Leeds, Brixton Hill, Ipswich, Bedford, Highbury, and Westminster, where his health failed and he had a year's rest at Bode in Cornwall, where one fruit of his residence is the neat and comely Methodist Chapel. He afterwards travelled in Launceston and Bristol, from whence he was appointed a colleague of the Rev. Hugh Price Hughes in the West Central Mission in London, of which the good results are well-known. As an author Mr. Pearce has a world-wide reputation, Cor- nish tales being his forte. Perhaps his most popular tale is that of the Cornish Cobbler "Daniel Quorm,"which has been translated into most of the continental languages and even into Chinese. His books are very popular in, the villages, and a good story is told in connection with his character sketches. On one occasion he was in a village in Lincolnshire, and was visiting some of the villagers. He came to one house where there was an old woman yho, upon learning who he was exclaimed : " So you are Mark Guy Pearse. Well I'm not going to say anything to you. You are not going to make a book out of me." Mr. Pearse is a very eloquent preacher and draws large congregations, and there is a treat in store for those who intend to hear him at Bewsey-road , Wesleyan Chapel on Wednesday next.

===== 0002601_18930415_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HULL STRIKE.

Exciting scenes were witnessed at Hull, on Thursday, in connection with the dock strike. Great crowds as- sembled at the Queen's and Prince's Docks, and a man who had been heard to declare that he would take a Federation ticket in spite of all the union men in Hull was assaulted, but was rescued by the police. Some boatsmen engaged in mooring a steamer were molested. Inquiries made at Bootle show that there is a strong pro- bability of many dock labourers and firemen coming out strike at the North-end Docks on Mond sy. Mr. J. H. Wilson, M.P.,speaking at Shields, on Thursday, invoked the unionists to make a firm stand, saying that if the Federation succeeded at Hull they intended extending their tactics elsewhere.

A formidable French squadron, composed of eight irmelads and fifteen cruisers and torpedo boats, arrived t Alexandria on Wednesday, and will probably remain thez• for about a week.
